# Architectural Decisions

Append-only log. One entry per decision. Reviewed at phase audits.

Format:
- **Decision N: [name]** (Package P, Session S)
  - Choice: [what was decided]
  - Reasoning: [one or two sentences]
  - Alternatives considered: [if relevant]

## Phase 1 baseline decisions

These were made before Session 1 and form the starting architecture.

- **D1: Tenancy model is database-per-tenant.** Strongest isolation. V1 runs one tenant locally; architecture is forward-built for many.
- **D2: No sub-workspaces inside a tenant in V1.** Tenant is the unit of organization. Workspaces deferred to V2 if needed.
- **D3: Identity is Keycloak in V1 Docker Compose.** Reference open-source IdP, OIDC + SAML + SCIM. ~1GB RAM cost accepted.
- **D4: LLM access via LiteLLM gateway.** No vendor SDKs in domain code. Ollama default in dev.
- **D5: Retrieval is hybrid, configured per agent.** Vector via pgvector and graph via Neo4j, both behind `RetrievalClient`. Strategy fixed at agent config in V1; runtime selection deferred to V2.
- **D6: Orchestration is LangGraph behind `AgentOrchestrator` interface.** CrewAI or custom orchestrators swappable later.
- **D7: Trace store is self-hosted Langfuse 3, behind an interface.** OpenTelemetry GenAI conventions used throughout.
- **D8: V1 metric scope is bounded.** Cache hit rate, output token distribution, context length trajectory, tool call patterns, cost per successful task, latency components decomposed, model substitution similarity scores. New metrics require documented decisions they inform.
- **D9: Optimization output is recommendation-shaped.** Every dashboard view ties to a recommended action.
- **D10: Stack versions pinned.** Python 3.14, Node 24 LTS, Postgres 17 with pgvector, Neo4j 5 Community, Keycloak 26, Redis 7, Vite 6, React 19, React Router 7, FastAPI, SQLAlchemy 2.0 async, LangGraph current, LiteLLM current, Langfuse 3.

## Session decisions

Appended as sessions produce them. New entries below this line.

---

- **D11: Scaffold grows incrementally** (Package P1, Session 1)
  - Choice: Each package adds the Compose services it requires. P1 ships repo structure, charter docs, and structural tooling. Compose at P1 close contains only services exercised by P1 or P2.
  - Reasoning: Big-bang scaffold front-loads complexity before any service has earned a use case, contradicting the learning-sprint framing of Phase 1. Incremental scaffold reaches agent work faster and prevents unused-service drift.
  - Alternatives considered: Big-bang scaffold (Postgres, Neo4j, Keycloak, Redis, LiteLLM, Langfuse all at P1 close). Rejected on learning-value and drift grounds.

- **D12: Jurisdiction is a first-class architectural attribute** (Pre-S2 charter edit)
  - Choice: Tenant context carries jurisdiction from P3 onward. Every component that touches customer data (databases, object storage, identity, trace store, LLM endpoints) is built to be regionally partitionable. Phase 1 deploys a single region; the architecture does not assume a single region anywhere in code.
  - Reasoning: Retrofitting jurisdiction across forty tables and every interface signature later is a quarter of work and a migration risk. Building it in from inception is free at the data-model layer and cheap at the interface layer. The discipline is "by construction, not by policy", which is the framing enterprise procurement and auditors require.
  - Alternatives considered: Treat jurisdiction as a Phase 2 concern and add it when the first non-default-region tenant arrives. Rejected on retrofit-cost grounds.

- **D13: Tenant onboarding is configuration, not deployment** (Pre-S2 charter edit)
  - Choice: Per-tenant decisions (jurisdiction, identity federation, classification policy, model endpoints, retention) live in the tenant registry as configuration. The application reads the registry; no code path hardcodes per-tenant behaviour. Adding a tenant to an existing regional stack is an idempotent provisioning workflow. Adding a region is a separate infrastructure event, scoped explicitly when a customer's residency requirement crosses an existing region boundary.
  - Reasoning: Onboarding through configuration scales; onboarding through deployment does not. The architectural commitment that adding a tenant requires no code changes is what makes the platform sellable to enterprise customers with custom IdP, jurisdiction, and classification requirements. Distinguishing tenant-add from region-add prevents over-claiming.
  - Alternatives considered: Conflate tenant and region provisioning into one workflow. Rejected because they have different actors, different lifecycles, and different cost profiles.

- **D14: Customer-specific behaviour is configuration; capability extensions are bounded** (Pre-S2 charter edit)
  - Choice: The platform exposes parameters, schemas, and tool integration points that cover the large majority of per-tenant requirements. Tools (external services called by the platform on the tenant's behalf) are the primary mechanism for customer-supplied logic and are configuration, not code. Extension points exist for the residual cases where logic must run inside the platform with access to internal state; extensions are versioned, sandboxed, and isolated per tenant, and are bounded to a small named set of interfaces (RetrievalClient, scorer, pre-processor). Forking the platform codebase for customer-specific logic is forbidden. (Cross-reference, added at S29b: for tools specifically: control-plane storage at Phase 1 per D89; per-tenant tool authoring activates in Phase 2 per the customer-deployment-evidence trigger named in D89's deferred-decisions entry.)
  - Reasoning: Configuration-only positions cannot survive contact with enterprise customers; codebase-forking destroys the platform. The middle path (configuration first, tool calls for most customer logic, sandboxed extensions for the residue) is the position every mature platform vendor converges on. Setting the boundary now prevents the rot.
  - Alternatives considered: Pure configuration with no extension surface (insufficient for enterprise). Open extension surface with arbitrary plugin points (becomes a fork farm). Rejected.

- **D15: Default development model is Qwen 2.5 7B** (Package P2, pre-S6)
  - Choice: Qwen 2.5 7B served via Ollama is the default model for development and the eval harness baseline.
  - Reasoning: Tool-calling fidelity matters once the agent surface lands, and Qwen 2.5's tool-call behaviour is more reliable than Llama 3.1 at comparable memory cost. Reversible if the eval harness surfaces problems.
  - Alternatives considered: Llama 3.1 8B (conservative default, weaker tool calling). Rejected on tool-call grounds.

- **D16: Codebase structure is bounded contexts on hexagonal, with platform / contexts / shared_kernel separation and import-linter enforcement** (Package P2, pre-S7)
  - Choice: The codebase organizes around bounded contexts at the top level (`contexts/`), with cross-cutting platform concerns (`platform/`), a strictly bounded `shared_kernel/`, and deployable units (`apps/`) that compose contexts and adapters into runnables. Each context internally follows hexagonal layers (`domain/`, `application/`, `ports/`, `adapters/inbound/`, `adapters/outbound/`). Boundaries are enforced by `import-linter` contracts in CI, not by convention. The `shared_kernel/` contains only types that must be referentially equal across contexts (TenantId, Jurisdiction) and forbids Pydantic imports to prevent framework version coupling. Packaging uses uv workspaces, with per-context `pyproject.toml` files added when a context first acquires a third-party dependency that should not bleed across boundaries. The observability split (`platform/observability/` is mechanism, `contexts/observability/` is intelligence) is deliberate: span emission is infrastructure every context uses; trace analysis and recommendation generation are a product feature with their own domain model.
  - Reasoning: Enterprise architecture review requires the answer to "where does retrieval live" to be a single folder, "how is multi-tenancy enforced" to be a build-time invariant, and "how do we swap a vendor" to be a port contract. Organizing by technical layer at the top level fails all three; organizing by bounded context with hexagonal layers within each passes all three. The platform / contexts / shared_kernel split keeps the import graph a DAG. Import-linter turns the architectural rules into CI failures rather than review comments.
  - Alternatives considered: Top-level layered structure (`domain/`, `application/`, `adapters/` at the root). Rejected: scatters each business concern across four directories and produces the modular-monolith-becomes-hairball failure mode. Top-level `ports/` directory. Rejected: ports are owned by the domain that needs them, not global. Pure flat package. Rejected: structurally indefensible at enterprise audit.

- **D17: Contexts communicate via published query APIs for reads and a domain event bus for state changes** (Package P2, pre-S7)
  - Choice: Each bounded context exposes a single `api.py` module at its root containing read-only query methods other contexts may call. State-changing interactions flow through a domain event bus in `platform/events/`: contexts publish events from their domain or application layer, other contexts subscribe through wiring in `apps/`. No context imports another context's internals. The bus is synchronous in-process in Phase 1; the interface is broker-ready so Phase 2 can swap to a real event broker without changing context code. `import-linter` enforces the rule that `contexts/X/*` may import only `contexts/Y/api`, never `contexts/Y/domain` or `contexts/Y/adapters`.
  - Reasoning: "Contexts communicate through ports or shared_kernel" is too vague to defend at audit. The hybrid (queries direct, commands eventful) is the pattern mature platform vendors converge on after the fact and is cheap to adopt now. It reflects Quorum's actual data flow: observability queries trace state on the hot path (read-shaped, latency-sensitive) and reacts to inference completions for recommendation generation (event-shaped, decoupled). Forcing reads through events adds latency without benefit; forcing writes through direct calls couples contexts that should stay separable.
  - Alternatives considered: Direct synchronous calls only (modular monolith, couples deployment). Events for everything (latency on the hot path, no benefit). Rejected on the trade-offs above.

- **D18: Redis is shared between Quorum application use and Langfuse ingestion in development; production Redis topology is deferred** (Package P2, Session 4)
  - Choice: A single Redis instance serves both Quorum application caching and queueing (DB 0) and Langfuse ingestion queueing (DB 1) in the local Compose stack. The configuration interface (per `platform/config/`, landing in S5) is structured so production deployments can resolve Quorum Redis and Langfuse Redis to either the same instance or separate instances by configuration, without code change.
  - Reasoning: Local development cost favours sharing; production posture depends on factors (queue depth, eviction policy compatibility, security boundary requirements) that are not yet known. Forcing a production decision now would commit on missing information. The architecture commits to the configuration interface that defers the decision cleanly.
  - Alternatives considered: Separate Redis instance for Langfuse from inception. Rejected: doubles development cost without benefit at this stage. Shared instance hardcoded with no config swap path. Rejected: forces production into a posture that may not fit.

- **D19: Configuration is read through `platform/config/` exclusively, no direct `os.getenv` or `.env` reads** (Package P2, Session 5)
  - Choice: A single `platform/config/` module is the only place secrets and environment-derived configuration enter the application. Pydantic Settings is the dev-time implementation, reading from `.env`. Production reads from a secret manager via the same `Settings` interface. `import-linter` contract forbids `os.getenv` outside `platform/config/`. Settings classes are per-concern (`InferenceSettings`, `ObservabilitySettings`, etc.), not god-objects.
  - Reasoning: "Secrets in `.env`" without an enforcement mechanism is a development convenience that pretends to be a security posture. The unified config interface is what makes "production swap is configuration not refactor" true on the secrets path. Pydantic Settings is chosen because it composes source priorities (env vars, dotenv, secret-manager-shaped backends) through a stable interface and is well-understood at audit.
  - Alternatives considered: Direct `os.getenv` calls (no enforcement, retrofits expensively). Per-context config modules (fragments the audit surface). Custom secrets layer (reinvents Pydantic Settings poorly).

- **D20: TLS configuration is read through `platform/config/`, dev profile defaults to plaintext for Compose-internal traffic, prod profile defaults to mTLS with no plaintext escape hatch** (Package P2, Session 5)
  - Choice: Every adapter that opens a network connection reads its TLS configuration through `platform/config/`. The dev profile resolves to plaintext for Compose-internal traffic (mkcert TLS at the Caddy edge stays in place). The production profile resolves to mTLS with private CA bundles. There is no code path that bypasses TLS in production; the production profile has no plaintext option.
  - Reasoning: The internal/external network distinction does not survive defence-in-depth review. ISO 27001 Annex A 8.20 explicitly requires controls on internal networks. Forcing TLS configuration through the unified config interface means the dev and production postures are profile selection, not code change.
  - Alternatives considered: Plaintext internal in production (rejected: fails Annex A 8.20). Per-adapter TLS configuration (rejected: fragments the audit surface). Service mesh (deferred: appropriate for production but premature in Phase 1).

- **D21: Field-level encryption uses envelope encryption via `platform/security/crypto.py`** (Package P2, Session 5)
  - Choice: Field-level encryption is implemented in `platform/security/crypto.py` using envelope encryption: a Key Encryption Key (KEK) wraps per-row Data Encryption Keys (DEKs); only DEKs touch field data. Field categories requiring crypto: per-tenant credentials, customer PII, audit log integrity hashes. The dev profile uses a fixed KEK from `platform/config/`. The production profile resolves the KEK to a KMS-managed key. The KMS vendor is deferred until production deployment context exists. Production crypto design is a candidate for explicit external review when production deployment context lands.
  - Reasoning: Disk-level encryption (platform-provided in production) protects against disk theft. Field-level encryption protects against database compromise where the attacker has DB credentials but not application crypto state. SOC 2 confidentiality, ISO 27001 Annex A 8.24, and sector frameworks like HIPAA all require field-level for sensitive categories. Envelope encryption is the standard pattern because it confines KEK use to the wrapping operation, allowing per-row key rotation without rewrapping the entire dataset.
  - Alternatives considered: Direct field encryption with a single key (rejected: no per-row rotation, larger blast radius). Application-transparent encryption via the database (rejected: couples to specific DB vendor and does not protect against DB credential compromise).

- **D22: Audit logging is a bounded context (`contexts/audit/`) with hash-chained append-only storage** (Package P2, Session 5)
  - Choice: Audit is a bounded context, not platform plumbing. `contexts/audit/` contains the domain model (AuditEvent value object, hash-chaining logic), the port (`AuditPort` for emitting events), and adapters. P2 ships with a no-op adapter for use in S6/S7. The real adapter (writing to a tenant-scoped append-only Postgres table with hash chaining) lands in P3 when the tenant registry exists. Schema: actor (user or system), tenant_id, jurisdiction, timestamp, action_verb, resource_type, resource_id, before_state (JSONB), after_state (JSONB), correlation_id, previous_event_hash, this_event_hash. Hash chain provides tamper-evidence: any insertion or modification breaks the chain at and after the affected entry, detectable by chain verification.
  - Reasoning: Audit data is tenant-scoped and has its own domain logic (querying, integrity verification, retention enforcement); it is a bounded context by the same criteria that make observability one. Hash chaining is the lowest-cost tamper-evidence mechanism that provides real audit value; full append-only storage with cryptographic signatures is overkill at this stage and can be added later by extending the chain logic. SOC 2 CC7.2, ISO 27001 Annex A 8.15, and sector framework per-record requirements all converge on this shape.
  - Alternatives considered: Audit as platform plumbing (rejected: tenant-scoping makes it a context). External audit service (rejected: introduces a dependency that does not earn its keep at Quorum's stage). No tamper-evidence (rejected: insufficient for SOC 2 and ISO 27001).

- **D23: Authentication and authorization are platform concerns; no endpoint ships without auth middleware** (Package P2, Session 5)
  - Choice: `platform/security/auth.py` provides the authentication interface. `platform/security/policy.py` provides the authorization interface. Both have dev backends (signed-token AuthN, single-tenant-single-role policy) and Keycloak-shaped production backends stubbed. The FastAPI surface in S7 ships with authentication middleware in front of every endpoint from the first commit. Policy checks gate every tenant-scoped operation. Privileged actions (admin operations, cross-tenant access) emit security events in addition to audit events.
  - Reasoning: Endpoints without authentication middleware ship in development and stay in production. The architectural rule is that the middleware is non-removable, enforced by an integration test that fails if any FastAPI route is reachable without it. Policy-as-code (rather than role-baked-into-handlers) is what makes per-tenant authorization tenable at scale.
  - Alternatives considered: Auth as middleware on a per-router basis (rejected: opt-in security is no security). Authorization in handlers (rejected: scatters the policy surface, fails audit). External authorization service (deferred: appropriate at scale, premature in Phase 1).

- **D24: Tenant isolation is verified by `tests/contract/tenant_isolation/` against every adapter touching tenant-scoped data** (Package P2, Session 5)
  - Choice: Cross-tenant access tests live in `tests/contract/tenant_isolation/`. They are red-team shaped: each test attempts unauthorized cross-tenant access and asserts the access fails. The harness is scaffolded in S5 with one example test against the no-op audit adapter. Every adapter that touches tenant-scoped data must have isolation tests in this directory. CI fails if a tenant-scoped adapter ships without corresponding isolation tests, enforced by a coverage rule (any module under `contexts/*/adapters/` referencing tenant_id must have a test under `tests/contract/tenant_isolation/`).
  - Reasoning: Tenant isolation by assertion is tenant isolation by hope. SOC 2 confidentiality, ISO 27001 Annex A 8.3, and the database-per-tenant principle (D1) all require verification, not declaration. Red-team shaped tests catch the failure mode the architecture is most exposed to: unintended cross-tenant data flow through poorly-scoped queries.
  - Alternatives considered: Manual review only (rejected: not durable). Property-based isolation testing (deferred: complementary, not replacement, and premature at Phase 1).

- **D25: Supply chain hardening: image digest pins, dependency lockfiles, vulnerability scanning, SBOM, scheduled monitoring of high-velocity dependencies** (Package P2, Session 5)
  - Choice: Container images pin to SHA256 digests in `compose.yaml` and any production manifests. Python dependencies pin via uv lockfile (lands in S7 with the FastAPI skeleton). Vulnerability scanning runs via `make scan`: Trivy against pulled images, `pip-audit` against Python deps. CI fails on critical CVEs (severity threshold configurable, default critical-only). SBOM generation is scaffolded as a Make target in S5 and emits real SBOMs from S7 onward. Scheduled dependency monitoring for high-velocity dependencies is part of supply chain hygiene; the first instance is monthly Langfuse patch checking, established at S4 close. Scheduled checks open digest-bump PRs with changelog excerpts and breaking-change flags; the operator reviews and merges; no auto-merge. Local CI runs via `make scan`; remote CI deferred to first remote deployment.
  - Reasoning: ISO 27001 Annex A 8.30 (outsourced development), 8.8 (vulnerability management), and SOC 2 CC8.1 (change management) all converge on supply chain controls. Digest pinning prevents image-tag mutation attacks and forces explicit upgrades. SBOMs are increasingly expected by enterprise procurement. Scheduled monitoring catches version drift before it becomes a CVE response.
  - Alternatives considered: Tag pinning only (rejected: tags mutate). External SCA service (deferred: appropriate at scale, premature now). No SBOM (rejected: enterprise procurement increasingly requires). Auto-merging dependency updates (rejected: removes operator judgment from a security-relevant change).

- **D26: Security events log separately from application logs via `platform/observability/security_events.py`** (Package P2, Session 5)
  - Choice: `platform/observability/security_events.py` provides a `SecurityEventLogger` interface. Event categories: auth_failure, authz_denial, config_change, tenant_scope_violation, privileged_action. Local backend writes structured JSON to `logs/security.jsonl` (separate from application log path, gitignored). Production backend ships to a SIEM, vendor deferred. Every auth middleware execution, every policy check, every audit log write also emits a security event for the corresponding category when applicable.
  - Reasoning: Application logs and security events have different lifecycles (application logs rotate, security events retain), different consumers (operators vs. security analysts vs. auditors), and different requirements (verbose vs. structured-and-filtered). Conflating them produces logs that are too verbose for SIEM and too sparse for debugging. SOC 2 CC7.2/CC7.3 and ISO 27001 Annex A 8.16 both require dedicated security monitoring.
  - Alternatives considered: Single log stream with severity tagging (rejected: SIEMs do not consume well, retention policies conflict). External security event service (deferred: appropriate at scale).

- **D27: OTel is the observability portability boundary; vendor-specific observability code is confined to adapters** (Package P2, Session 5)
  - Choice: All observability emission in `platform/observability/` and in `contexts/*/` uses OpenTelemetry APIs with GenAI semantic conventions. Langfuse-specific code is confined to `contexts/observability/adapters/outbound/langfuse/` and contains only the OTLP exporter configuration and the trace-query adapter for the read path. The OTLP collector endpoint is read through `platform/config/`. Switching to a different OTel-consuming observability tool is a configuration change plus a trace-query adapter rewrite.
  - Reasoning: D7 commits to self-hosted Langfuse 3 with OTel GenAI conventions. The portability claim that follows from that commitment needs to be explicit and architecturally enforced, or it decays into vendor lock-in by accretion. OTel as the wire protocol is the portability mechanism; the adapter pattern is the structural enforcement. OTel has consolidated as the cross-vendor observability standard, meeting the optionality principle's threshold for committed adoption.
  - Alternatives considered: Langfuse SDK directly in domain code (rejected: vendor lock-in by import). OTel emission with no architectural constraint on Langfuse-specific code (rejected: Langfuse-specific code spreads, swap cost rises).

- **D28: Python package is named `zephyr` to decouple implementation namespace from product name and resolve stdlib collision** (Package P2, pre-S6)
  - Choice: The top-level Python package is named `zephyr` (Esperanto for "north," continuing the navigation-theme lineage of the Quorum project name). The product name "Quorum" remains in user-facing surfaces, charter strategic framing, and external references; the package name `zephyr` is the implementation namespace. From this commit forward, charter decisions and working docs refer to `zephyr/config/`, `zephyr/security/`, `zephyr/observability/` as the file-path locations of the cross-cutting layer. Historical D-entries (D16, D19–D27) retain their original `platform/` wording as append-only record; the architectural concept they describe is unchanged.
  - Reasoning: The original `platform/` package name collides with Python's stdlib `platform` module, requiring a `conftest.py` shim as workaround. Trademark uncertainty around "Quorum" makes a package name tied to the product name a future-rebranding risk. Decoupling the package name from the product name protects against both: stdlib collision goes away with the rename, and a future product rename does not require touching every Python file.
  - Alternatives considered: `core_platform` (still contains the colliding word, generic, weak). `quorum` (couples package to potentially-changing product name). Other navigation-theme names (`compass`, `bearing`) lack the personal resonance and language-specific distinctiveness.

- **D29: Product name changes from Quorum to Zephyr** (Package P2, post-S6)
  - Choice: The product name is Zephyr from this commit forward. The package name was already `zephyr` per D28; the product and package names now align. User-facing surfaces (README, charter strategic framing, future UI copy, customer-facing communications) refer to Zephyr. Historical D-entries and session log reflections retain their original "Quorum" wording per the append-only principle in `principles.md`. The original D28 text, which committed to Quorum as the user-facing product name, stands as written.
  - Reasoning: The trademark uncertainty around "Quorum" cited in D28's reasoning has resolved, and the package-name decoupling D28 introduced means the product rename touches user-facing surfaces, charter narrative, operational defaults, and one config class name, but not the import namespace. "Zephyr" (Esperanto for "north") carries personal resonance for the operator and continues the navigation-theme of the package name. The decision is recorded as a charter D-entry rather than absorbed silently because future audits need a single locatable explanation for why the product name changes between commits visible in git history.
  - Alternatives considered: In-place edits to historical D-entries and session reflections (rejected: violates the append-only principle in `principles.md`, destroys audit-trail value, and the "one-time exception" framing is precisely how append-only erodes). Carrying forward both names in user-facing surfaces during a transition period (rejected: the project is pre-customer, no external surface depends on either name, clean break is cheaper than dual-naming).

- **D30: uv workspace topology — root pyproject is the cross-cutting platform package; bounded contexts and apps are workspace members** (Package P2, Session 7)
  - Choice: The repo-root `pyproject.toml` represents the `zephyr` cross-cutting platform package: it carries the runtime dependencies for `zephyr/config/`, `zephyr/security/`, `zephyr/observability/`, `zephyr/events/`, and `shared_kernel/`. Bounded contexts (`contexts/inference`, `contexts/observability`, `contexts/audit`) and deployable apps (`apps/api`) are uv workspace members declared in `[tool.uv.workspace]`, each with its own `pyproject.toml` declaring only the third-party dependencies that context or app uses. Members use `[tool.uv] package = false` so they are dependency-tracked but not built as wheels in dev — Python imports continue to resolve through `pythonpath = ["."]` in the root pytest config, which keeps the import-linter root packages and the AST enforcement walkers unchanged. Cross-context imports continue to be policed by import-linter (D16, D17) regardless of workspace topology; the workspace topology adds the second layer of enforcement at the dependency-manifest level.
  - Reasoning: The bounded-context boundary (D16) is enforced at the import level by import-linter; the workspace topology adds a second, complementary layer of enforcement at the dependency level. Declaring `litellm` only in `contexts/inference/pyproject.toml` and `langfuse` only in `contexts/observability/pyproject.toml` makes a vendor SDK leaking into another context a manifest-resolution failure, not just an import-linter failure. Keeping `zephyr/` (and `shared_kernel/`) at the workspace root rather than as members of their own avoids further pyproject proliferation for cross-cutting code that has small, shared third-party deps and is referenced by every context anyway. Apps land as members because each app is a distinct deployable unit with its own dependency profile (FastAPI for `apps/api/`, Vite for the future UI app, etc.).
  - Alternatives considered: Each cross-cutting concern (`zephyr/config`, `zephyr/security`, `zephyr/observability`, `zephyr/events`) as its own workspace member (rejected: forces pyproject proliferation without dependency-scoping benefit, since zephyr/ runtime deps are small and uniformly shared by every context). Only a root pyproject with no members and dependency-groups instead (rejected: defeats the per-context dependency-scoping commitment, leaves boundary enforcement only at the import level). `apps/api/` as a non-packaged consumer outside the workspace (rejected: loses uv's editable-install benefits for dev iteration on the app while still depending on workspace members).

- **D31: Package-level archive document at every package close from P2 forward** (Package P2, retrospective)
  - Choice: Every package from P2 onward produces an archive document at package close, written to `docs/archive/packages/pN.md`. The archive contains the package goal, sessions shipped, architectural commitments landed, stack state at close, data surfaced, drift surfaced, durable lessons, what the package leaves to the next, and convention changes. P1 retains no archive (the audit-time-only convention applied at P1 close, before this convention was settled). The cost calibration of the per-package archive convention is revisited at the Phase 1 close audit; if archive documents prove ceremonial in P3 through P5, the convention is reviewed.
  - Reasoning: Mid-phase package retrospectives produce synthesis with standalone value. P1 was structurally light enough that the absence of an archive has not bitten; P2 produced 16 D-entries, two AST tests, fourteen import-linter contracts, one rebrand, three durable lessons, and four drift candidates, which is enough material that the synthesis has audit value beyond the session log entries. Locating the synthesis across S4 through S8 session entries every time someone asks is reconstruction work the operator pays repeatedly. The archive document is the cheapest way to make that work payable once. The wording in `principles.md` ("Old content moves to archive at audit time, never deletes") becomes more permissive: "audit time" now includes package close from P2 forward. The principles wording itself is reviewed at phase audit if the convention sticks.
  - Alternatives considered: Defer all archives to phase audit (rejected: P2's material has standalone value that erodes if locked behind a phase audit twelve sessions away). Archive only at certain packages chosen ad hoc (rejected: ceremony question gets re-asked every package; better to settle the convention and revisit at phase audit). Treat the session log as the archive (rejected: session log entries are session-shaped, not package-shaped, and force per-audit reconstruction).

- **D32: Per-tenant Postgres instance topology — separate instances, two at P3 open for the test set, instance creation deferred until production deployment context** (Package P3, Session 9)
  - Choice: Each tenant has its own Postgres instance. P3 opens with two instances (`postgres-tenant-a`, `postgres-tenant-b`) seeded as tenants in the registry, joining a dedicated `postgres-control-plane` instance per D33. The provisioning workflow (registry insert, instance connection, control-plane row creation, schema migration) is exercised against these existing instances rather than against instance creation. Adding tenants beyond the two in Phase 1 requires editing Compose; instance creation as an automated step lands when production deployment context arrives and infrastructure-as-code is real. (Cross-reference, added at S29b: for tools specifically: control-plane storage at Phase 1 per D89; per-tenant tool authoring activates in Phase 2 per the customer-deployment-evidence trigger.)
  - Reasoning: D1 commits to database-per-tenant; the topology question (single instance with database-per-tenant logical separation versus separate instances) was open. The operator chose separate instances at the P2 retrospective on enterprise-grade learning grounds: running multiple Postgres instances locally exercises the routing layer under realistic conditions and makes the database-per-tenant claim concrete rather than theoretical. The cost is one more Postgres instance per tenant in the local Compose stack and a more substantial routing layer (per-tenant connection details in the registry, credential encryption per D21). Two test tenants at P3 open is sufficient to exercise the routing, isolation, and migration paths without committing to a dynamic-Compose pattern that has limited production analogue.
  - Alternatives considered: Single Postgres instance with logical database-per-tenant separation (rejected: lower local cost but does not exercise the routing layer or the credential-decryption path under realistic conditions; the architectural commitment to enterprise grade favours the more substantial routing). One instance at P3 open with provisioning workflow creating instances dynamically (rejected: dynamic-Compose pattern in dev does not match production posture, where instances are created by infrastructure-as-code; the local-versus-production gap is harder to absorb than the current Option A breach of D13 which is recoverable when production deployment lands).

- **D33: Control-plane database lives on a dedicated Postgres instance, separate from tenant data planes** (Package P3, Session 9)
  - Choice: The tenant registry and any other control-plane data live on a dedicated `postgres-control-plane` Postgres instance. Tenant data planes are entirely separate per-tenant Postgres instances per D32. The control plane and tenant data planes never share a Postgres instance.
  - Reasoning: Control-plane data and tenant data are operationally different. Control-plane data is jurisdiction-spanning (the registry has rows for tenants in every jurisdiction Zephyr serves), operator-owned, and operator-administered. Tenant data is jurisdiction-local (each per-tenant database lives in the tenant's jurisdiction), tenant-owned, and operator-administered on the tenant's behalf. Sharing a Postgres instance between them couples the operational lifecycle of the control plane to the operational lifecycle of one tenant's data plane and creates an audit comment ("why does Tenant A's database server also host the global tenant registry?") that the architecture should not have to answer. The D18 Redis-sharing precedent does not apply: D18 shared because Redis caching and Langfuse ingestion are operationally similar (both queueing); control-plane and tenant data planes are operationally distinct.
  - Alternatives considered: Control-plane data on the existing P1 Postgres instance currently serving Zephyr application data (rejected: the existing instance was set up as Zephyr application Postgres; co-locating control-plane on it conflates two operational roles and creates the same audit-comment shape as the D32 alternative). Control-plane data on a per-region Postgres instance shared with tenants in that region (rejected: still couples lifecycles, only at regional rather than per-tenant granularity, and adds a region-cardinality complication to the control-plane).

- **D34: Credential encryption integration shape and three-control leak prevention** (Package P3, Session 10)
  - Choice: The tenant registry adapter integrates envelope encryption from `zephyr/security/crypto.py` (D21) on the credential write and read paths. Plaintext `TenantConnectionConfig` enters the adapter as input to `register_tenant`, is wrapped via `encrypt_field(plaintext_bytes, context={"tenant_id": ..., "purpose": "tenant.credentials.v1"})` before any I/O, and only the encrypted bytes touch Postgres. Read-back reverses the path: rows return `EncryptedCredentials`; the use case at the application layer unwraps to `TenantConnectionConfig` only when an operator-context caller invokes `reveal_connection_config`. Plaintext lives only in function-local variables, never in instance state, never in logs. AAD binds `tenant_id` and a fixed purpose string `"tenant.credentials.v1"` to prevent ciphertext replay across tenants. The wire shape from `zephyr/security/crypto.py` (`EncryptedField` with `wrapped_dek`, `dek_wrap_nonce`, `ciphertext`, `nonce`, `key_version`) is persisted directly onto the row (eleven columns total: five crypto-wire columns plus `aad` plus five identity/metadata columns — the S9 prompt's "eight columns" estimate predated reconciling against the live `crypto.py` wire shape and is corrected here); the domain `EncryptedCredentials` value object carries the operationally meaningful triplet (`wrapped_dek`, `ciphertext`, `aad`), and the adapter handles the wire-shape↔domain-shape conversion internally so the domain stays portable. The adapter persists the AAD bytes redundantly (also recomputable from `tenant_id`+purpose) so the domain object is self-contained for cross-tenant-replay tests; on decrypt the adapter recomputes AAD from the row's `tenant_id` to guarantee the binding holds even if the stored AAD is tampered with. Three leak-prevention controls land in the same commits as the integration: (a) `zephyr/observability/credential_scrub.py` provides `CredentialScrubFilter` registered on the root logger, dropping records whose `extra` dict references plaintext credential field names or whose message string matches plaintext field-name patterns; (b) `tests/_enforcement/test_no_plaintext_in_state.py` walks `contexts/tenancy/adapters/` via AST and asserts no class definition declares an instance attribute typed as `TenantConnectionConfig`; (c) `tests/contract/tenant_isolation/test_registry_isolation.py` red-team-tests cross-tenant credential reads and operator-context-only access policy. Audit destination for registry mutations: `AuditEvent.tenant_id=""` (the empty-string sentinel, since the dataclass field is typed `str` not `str | None`) indicates control-plane scope; S12's audit adapter routes such events to a control-plane audit table on the control-plane database, with the same hash-chained schema as per-tenant tables. One audit port, two routing destinations at the adapter. Policy shape: credential-revealing operations reject *all* tenant-context callers (own-tenant included) at the use-case policy boundary, accepting only operator-context callers; the S11 connection-resolution layer runs as an operator-context system actor and is the only consumer S10 supports. Alembic topology: single `alembic.ini` at the repo root with named `[alembic:control_plane]` section, separate `alembic/control_plane/` script directory holding `env.py` and `versions/`. S11 adds `[alembic:tenant]` and `alembic/tenant/` without restructuring; `make migrate` invokes `alembic --name control_plane upgrade head`. SQLAlchemy 2.0 async usage in the adapter is Core-shaped: `create_async_engine`, `async_sessionmaker(expire_on_commit=False)`, and `Table`/`MetaData` plus `select`/`insert`/`update` statements; the row→domain conversion is manual inside the adapter rather than `registry.map_imperatively(Tenant, table)`, because SQLAlchemy mutates instances when loading rows and the `Tenant` aggregate is a frozen dataclass. The frozen-aggregate-plus-Core pattern is the right shape for hexagonal: the adapter owns the impedance mismatch, the domain stays pure, no ORM identity map tax, and decryption happens in Python after the row is fetched (asyncpg's prepared-statement cache returns bytea as `bytes` regardless of cache state, so caching is irrelevant to decryption correctness).
  - Reasoning: D21 commits to envelope encryption as the pattern; S10 is where it moves from library code to integrated production path. The three-control structure is defense-in-depth around the structural discipline that plaintext never persists. The AST test makes future plaintext-in-state regressions a CI failure, not a review comment. The AAD binding is what makes envelope encryption tenant-safe rather than merely cryptographically sound: without AAD binding, a stolen wrapped DEK from tenant A's row could decrypt tenant A's ciphertext if reattached to tenant B's row; with AAD binding to tenant_id and purpose, the InvalidTag boundary catches the misuse. Audit destination separation matches D33's operational-lifecycle distinction: control-plane mutations are operator-context, jurisdiction-spanning, and do not belong in any single tenant's audit chain. Operator-context-only credential reads keep the policy surface bounded; no use case has demanded tenant-self-credential-read, and adding the boundary later is cheaper than removing it. The Core-not-ORM choice is forced by the frozen-dataclass shape S9 settled (StrEnum + bytes + datetime + frozen) — `map_imperatively` would force unfrozen instances or hand-rolled `__setattr__` shenanigans, neither of which is a defensible domain shape; manual row→domain construction in the adapter is fifteen lines and aligns with the principle that adapters own the impedance mismatch.
  - Alternatives considered: Plaintext credentials in Postgres with disk-level encryption only (rejected: D21 commits to field-level for credentials specifically; disk encryption does not protect against DB credential compromise). Separate `AuditOperatorPort` for control-plane mutations (rejected: doubles audit surface for no architectural gain; one port with adapter-level routing is sufficient). Tenant-self-credential-read at the policy layer (rejected: doubles policy surface for an unrequested feature; reversible if a use case emerges). AAD without tenant binding (rejected: leaves replay surface). Separate `alembic.ini` files per track (rejected: forces `make migrate` to chain two `alembic -c <path>` invocations rather than two `--name <name>` invocations against one config; the cookbook pattern is the canonical shape and keeps the operator surface tight). `registry.map_imperatively(Tenant, table)` on the existing frozen-dataclass aggregate (rejected: SQLAlchemy mutates instances on load, incompatible with `frozen=True`; either unfreeze the aggregate or hand-roll instance construction in the adapter — the latter is fifteen lines and preserves the domain shape).

- **D35: Audit destination convention promoted from S10 reflection** (Package P3, Session 11)
  - Choice: `AuditEvent.tenant_id` carries an empty-string sentinel `""` to indicate control-plane scope; non-empty `tenant_id` indicates tenant-scoped scope. The empty-string sentinel rather than `None` reflects the audit domain's existing typing (`tenant_id: str`, established before D34's framing); the domain type is not retroactively widened. The audit adapter at S12 routes events at adapter entry with one if-statement: empty `tenant_id` writes to a control-plane audit table on the control-plane database; non-empty writes to a per-tenant audit table on the routed tenant data plane. Hash chains are per-destination: control-plane events form one chain on the control-plane audit table; each tenant forms its own chain on its per-tenant audit table. The genesis `previous_event_hash` for both chain types is the sentinel `"0" * 64`. One `AuditPort` exposes both destinations; no separate `AuditOperatorPort`.
  - Reasoning: D34 forecast `tenant_id=None` for control-plane audit events. S10's wiring of the registry adapter's audit emissions surfaced the typing mismatch (audit domain was `str`-typed, not `str | None`); the empty-string sentinel was the pragmatic choice and held cleanly through the round-trip and isolation tests. Promoting the convention from reflection to D-entry is the right shape for a constraint S12 inherits: an inheriting session reading a D-entry is stronger than reading a reflection paragraph. The per-destination chain is the only design that preserves D22's tamper-evidence guarantee under the routing logic; a single global chain across destinations would require cross-database hash coordination which doesn't survive D32's per-tenant instance topology.
  - Alternatives considered: Widen `AuditEvent.tenant_id` to `str | None` (rejected at S10: retroactive domain change for a sentinel that empty-string handles cleanly; the audit context's hash-chain helpers already accept `tenant_id` as `str`). Separate `AuditOperatorPort` for control-plane events (rejected at D34: doubles the audit surface). Single global hash chain across all destinations (rejected: requires cross-database coordination that contradicts D32's instance independence; per-destination chains are independent and cleanly verifiable).

- **D36: Per-tenant connection routing layer with cached session factories and two-phase migration runner** (Package P3, Session 11)
  - Choice: The connection routing layer lives in `contexts/tenancy/` and is exposed via `contexts/tenancy/api.get_tenant_session_factory(tenant_id, principal)`. The implementation in `contexts/tenancy/application/connection_resolution.py` composes the registry's `reveal_connection_config` use case with an `AsyncEngine` factory adapter at `contexts/tenancy/adapters/outbound/sqlalchemy/session_factory.py`. Per-tenant `AsyncEngine` instances are cached at the routing layer keyed on `tenant_id`; cache invalidation hooks fire on `update_tenant_status` (any status transition disposes the cached engine). The cache is process-local; in production multi-replica deployments, each replica maintains its own cache and invalidation propagates through registry-update events (deferred to event-bus integration; in S11, single-replica dev makes this a non-issue). Operator-context principal is required at the api function signature; tenant-context callers raise `AuthorizationError` at the use-case policy boundary. The two-phase migration runner orchestrates control-plane first, per-tenant second; the per-tenant phase iterates over registered tenants in the registry and applies the per-tenant Alembic track against each tenant's database. Per-tenant phase is per-tenant transactional: failure on tenant B leaves tenant A migrated and tenant B at its prior version; retry resumes from tenant B. No cross-tenant atomicity at the migration layer (cross-database distributed transactions contradict D32's instance independence). Idempotency is Alembic-native: re-running `make migrate` after no schema changes is a no-op via the version table. The per-tenant initial schema contains the `tenant_audit` table per D22 schema, identical across tenants in S11; tenant-specific configuration (classification policy, retention) lives in tenant-configuration tables that are themselves part of the standard per-tenant schema, not in schema variations. Reconciliation findings recorded inline: SQLAlchemy 2.0 — `create_async_engine(url, pool_pre_ping=True)` with conservative per-tenant pool sizing (`pool_size=2, max_overflow=3`) keeps total connections tight in dev (≤2 tenants × 5 max = 10 peak); `async_sessionmaker(engine, expire_on_commit=False)` is the binding shape; `await engine.dispose()` is the lifecycle call for cache eviction (S10's adapter already uses this on shutdown). Alembic — programmatic invocation uses `Config(file, ini_section=name)`, `cfg.set_main_option("sqlalchemy.url", per_tenant_url)`, `command.upgrade(cfg, "head")`; the bracket-form `[NAME]` named-section pattern from S10 is compatible with both CLI `--name` invocations and programmatic `ini_section=` constructs; topology choice is two-config (separate `alembic/tenant/` directory parallel to `alembic/control_plane/`) per S11 framing. asyncpg pool — SQLAlchemy 2.0's `pool_pre_ping=True` is the right idiom for stale-connection handling under `make down`/`make up` cycles; no further mitigation needed. SQLAlchemy import-linter scope: a parallel `sqlalchemy-confined-tenancy` contract was considered but not added — the routing layer's application-level cache holds `async_sessionmaker` instances and benefits from typing those values, and `domain-purity` already forbids SQLAlchemy in domain code; tenancy.application/ legitimately uses SQLAlchemy types as port-shaped opaque values, so no new contract was added (lint contract count remains 15).
  - Reasoning: The routing layer's home in the tenancy context follows D17's api.py convention: "give me a session for tenant X" is functionally a read against the registry plus session construction, and the tenancy context owns both halves. Cross-cutting placement under `zephyr/persistence/` was considered and rejected because it would invert the dependency direction (zephyr/ depending on contexts/) without architectural benefit. Cached factories rather than per-request construction keeps the KEK-unwrap path off the request hot path and prevents `reveal_connection_config` security event volume from becoming audit noise; cache invalidation on status transitions is the minimum-surface invalidation policy that handles the only mutation paths registered tenants currently support. Per-tenant transactional semantics rather than cross-tenant atomicity reflect the D32 commitment: each tenant's data plane is operationally independent, and the migration runner cannot promise atomicity it cannot deliver. Initial schema as the audit table specifically (rather than a placeholder) is justified by S12 needing it anyway; landing the schema in S11 lets S12 focus on the audit adapter implementation rather than schema-plus-adapter together. Identical schema across tenants in S11 reflects classification policy and retention being tenant-configuration not schema differences.
  - Alternatives considered: Routing layer in `zephyr/persistence/` (rejected: inverts dependency direction; tenancy context already owns the registry and decryption, owning session construction extends that responsibility cleanly). Per-request factory construction (rejected: hot-path KEK unwrap, security event noise). Cache invalidation on every read (rejected: defeats caching). Cross-tenant migration atomicity via two-phase commit or Saga (rejected: contradicts D32 instance independence; per-tenant transactional with retry is sufficient). Placeholder initial schema (rejected: throwaway work, S12 needs the audit table anyway).


- **D37: Audit adapter shape with hash-chain concurrency control and cache deferred** (Package P3, Session 12)
  - Choice: The audit Postgres adapter implements `AuditPort` against per-destination Postgres tables. Routing at adapter entry per D35: empty-string `tenant_id` writes to the control-plane `tenant_audit` table on the control-plane database; non-empty `tenant_id` writes to the routed tenant's per-tenant `tenant_audit` table via `get_tenant_session_factory`. Hash chaining: each INSERT is preceded by a `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` against the destination table's chain-tail row inside the same transaction (`async with session.begin()`); the SELECT-then-INSERT race is closed by row-level locking at the default READ COMMITTED isolation level. The previous-hash-cache mentioned in S11 framing is deferred: SELECT-before-INSERT remains the correct shape until measurement justifies caching. The `tenant_id` field is denormalised onto each event row for query convenience even though per-tenant tables sit on per-tenant databases (the routing context is implicit in destination, but the field is available for cross-database aggregation queries when those land). OTel spans on audit-emitting code paths carry `tenant.id` and `tenant.jurisdiction` as span attributes; the namespace is Zephyr-domain because the OTel semantic-conventions group has not stabilised multi-tenant attribute naming as of 2026-04-30 reconciliation (de-facto practice in early-2026 guidance is `tenant.id` for both resource and span attributes, but it is not in the stable semconv spec). If the OTel group converges on a different namespace, the migration is a span-attribute rename and a one-line update at the emission site. Cross-destination CHECK constraints land at schema layer: per-tenant `tenant_audit` CHECK enforces non-empty `tenant_id`; control-plane `tenant_audit` CHECK enforces empty-string `tenant_id`. Accidental cross-destination writes raise constraint violations rather than silently corrupting destination chains.
  - Reasoning: The SELECT-then-INSERT race is real even at single-replica scale (concurrent requests in the same process can interleave) and the hash-chain integrity guarantee from D22 cannot survive a known race. SELECT FOR UPDATE inside the transaction is the lowest-overhead concurrency-safe shape Postgres offers and matches the per-row lifecycle of a hash chain. Caching the last-written hash in adapter memory is faster but trades correctness for performance without measurement to justify the trade; deferring the cache means the architectural posture is correct-by-construction and a future optimisation lands when needed. Zephyr-domain attribute names for tenant identity reflect the OTel-as-portability-boundary commitment from D27: emitting via OTel is the portability claim, the specific attribute names are domain choices that travel through OTel cleanly. Denormalising `tenant_id` onto the event row costs nothing at write time and keeps cross-database aggregation queries (audit dashboards, compliance reporting) straightforward when those land. The schema-level CHECK constraints make accidental cross-destination writes fail fast at the database layer, defense-in-depth around the application-layer routing.
  - Alternatives considered: Cache the last-written hash in adapter memory (rejected: trades correctness for unmeasured performance; deferred until measurement justifies). Serialisable isolation level on the audit transaction (rejected: heavier than needed; SELECT FOR UPDATE on the chain-tail row is sufficient). Advisory locks for concurrency control (rejected: more complex than row-level locking and adds a second mechanism alongside the existing transaction model). Omit `tenant_id` from per-tenant rows (rejected: cross-database queries need it; denormalisation cost is zero). Drop the cross-destination CHECK constraints (rejected: schema-layer defense complements the routing if-statement at zero runtime cost).

- **D38: Project reframe as learning sprint and rename zephyr → padhanam** (Package P3, Session 13)
  - Choice: The project is reframed from "agentic workflow SaaS platform under build" to "personal learning sprint executed under enterprise-realistic constraints." The architectural commitments (SOC 2 Type II and ISO 27001 compliance targets, database-per-tenant tenancy, hash-chained audit, envelope encryption, supply-chain hardening, jurisdiction as a first-class attribute, OTel as observability portability boundary, all D-entries D1-D37) stand unchanged; the constraints are now correctly framed as the curriculum the operator is learning, not as commercial-platform requirements. Prior D-entries' reasoning citing "enterprise procurement," "enterprise customers," or "what makes the platform sellable" is now read as "what enterprises require from systems they buy or build internally," which is the substrate the operator wants fluency in. The reasoning is factually correct as originally written; only the framing it sat inside has changed. The project is renamed from zephyr to padhanam (Esperanto for "learning"). The package namespace is renamed `zephyr/` → `padhanam/`. Project name and package name converge again; D28's package-name-decoupled-from-product-name rationale (commercial-rebrand-protection) no longer applies because there is no commercial product identity to protect against future rebranding of. Historical D-entries and session log reflections retain their original wording per the append-only principle.
  - Reasoning: The current Bet's framing as "an agentic workflow SaaS platform" with "commercial differentiator" framing was inconsistent with the operator's actual purpose, which has always been personal learning under enterprise-realistic constraints. The inconsistency was not load-bearing for any architectural decision (every D-entry's reasoning is grounded in what enterprises actually require, regardless of commercial framing), but it was inconsistent enough to mislead future strategic conversations and prospective collaborators reading the charter. The reframe corrects the framing to match the actual purpose. The rename to padhanam reflects the learning-sprint framing in the project's identity itself; the Esperanto name continues the operator's personal-resonance lineage from zephyr. Renaming and reframing in one D-entry rather than two reflects that the rename is consequent to the reframe; doing them separately would imply they are independent decisions, which they are not.
  - Alternatives considered: Reframe without renaming (rejected: the project name "Zephyr" carried platform-product weight that the reframe makes inappropriate; the name needs to align with the new framing). Rename without reframing (rejected: would produce new directory names but leave the Bet claiming commercial intent, requiring this work to redo at the next strategic conversation). Edit historical D-entries and session log reflections in place to remove commercial framing (rejected: violates the append-only principle, destroys audit-trail value, and is exactly the erosion S8's reflection on D29 explicitly flagged as the failure mode of "one-time exception" framing). Archive the existing charter wholesale and start fresh (rejected: the architectural commitments stand unchanged, and the audit trail value of the existing decisions log is preserved by the reframe-without-rewrite shape). Use a different package name from the project name to preserve D28's decoupling (rejected: D28's decoupling was specifically commercial-rebrand-protection, which doesn't apply to a learning sprint; convergence is cleaner).

 ## D[39]: Project framing reframed from learning sprint to product-led AI-assisted enterprise development case study

**Context.** `bet.md` rewritten in full to describe the actual proposition being investigated. The operating model is now explicitly a senior product leader directing implementation through Claude Code, with the methodology as the proprietary insight and the platform as the public demonstration. Prior framing as "personal learning sprint" understated what was being built and why; the reframe makes the bet honest about the audience (senior product leaders, CPOs, consultancies) and the deliverable (the methodology, demonstrated through the platform).

**Architectural impact.** None. Existing decisions D1 through D[current] stand unchanged; their technical reasoning was never dependent on the learning-sprint framing.

**Documentation impact.**
- `charter/bet.md` rewritten in full.
- `charter/methodology.md` added as a new document, descriptive at this stage. Captures the product-leader-and-implementer pattern as it surfaces during sessions; will mature toward prescriptive at a phase audit.
- `charter/deferred-decisions.md` data-plane-ownership block rewritten to give an architectural reason for the commitment rather than a commercial-IP reason. Other deferred-decision blocks unchanged.
- `README.md` opening paragraph and "How to read the charter" section updated; `methodology.md` added to the reading order.
- `CLAUDE.md` gains a sub-section on methodology capture in session log entries.

**Operating model impact.** Session log entries should now surface methodology observations briefly under a "methodology" line when the session exercises something about the architect-implementer pattern itself (a brief format that worked or didn't, a class of drift caught and corrected, a moment where the model and product leader diverged on approach). Accumulated observations are promoted into `methodology.md` upstream at audit boundaries. 

## D[40]: Methodology measured against DORA Four Keys and CORE4 dimensions

**Context.** The methodology is the proprietary insight that the case study is testing. Measuring it against the established frameworks for software delivery performance produces evidence in the form the engineering and product leadership audience already recognises. Anecdotal demonstrations are common in the AI-assisted development space; quantitative evidence at this scale, sustained over months, in public, is rare and is the differentiating asset.

**Cadence.** Capture every session, compute every package close, trend-analyse every phase audit. Detail in `methodology.md` under the Measurement section.

**Public reporting.** Metrics are reported publicly as part of the case study, including when the numbers do not flatter the methodology. Honesty is the discipline.

**Operational impact.**
- `methodology.md` gains a Measurement section describing the dimensions, definitions, and cadence.
- `methodology.md` gains a session log tagging format that becomes the source data for all downstream computation.
- `deferred-decisions.md` gains a "Methodology metrics" entry covering the deferred operational commitments (tooling, format, benchmarks).
- Session log entries from this point forward include the structured tagging block.
- Package retrospectives in `packages.md` gain a measured-outcomes paragraph at each package close.
- Phase audits in `audits.md` gain a measured-outcomes section at each phase audit.

**Architectural impact.** None. The platform is unchanged; the measurement is of the methodology, not of the platform's runtime characteristics.

- **D41: Cost capture and per-tenant attribution committed as Phase 1 architectural commitment** (Package P3, post-close strategic session)
  - Choice: Cost capture lands across Phase 1 packages as follows. P4 framing: pricing table in `padhanam/config/inference.py`, an OTel attribute extension on the inference adapter joining token counts to USD, and trace-handler wiring producing `gen_ai.cost.input_usd`, `gen_ai.cost.output_usd`, and `gen_ai.cost.total_usd` attributes. P4 schema migration: a per-tenant cost-attribution column added via Alembic to the relevant control-plane registry surfaces (this is a retrofit relative to the original preference for landing the column at P3 open; P3 is now closed and the column lands at P4 setup before cost-bearing inference begins to flow). P5 evaluation harness: cost-per-successful-task metric implementation per D8's existing commitment. P11: cost-aware optimization recommendations as a first-class surface on the recommendation API. The pricing table is reviewed via `ops/scheduled_checks.yaml` cadence (vendor pricing updates checked monthly). Cost ceilings, multi-tier model routing, and progressive throttling are deferred to Phase 2 in `deferred-decisions.md`.
  - Reasoning: D8 commits cost-per-successful-task as a Phase 1 metric; the work to mechanically realise the commitment was therefore already in scope and treating cost as a Phase 2 concern was inconsistent with D8 as written. Cost is the economic dimension that completes the optimization-as-recommendation differentiator from D9; without cost, recommendations can say "this is faster" or "this is higher-quality" but never "this costs N% more for M% quality at the same task type." Per-tenant attribution as a column rather than as a downstream aggregation keeps the audit answer to "what did this tenant cost" a single SELECT, which matters for any tenant-cost reporting that lands in P9 onwards. The retrofit at P4 instead of at P3 open is the recoverable cost; the alternative — leaving cost attribution to be migrated when production traffic surfaces a problem — would compound, and the case study's posture treats avoidable retrofit as a learning failure rather than a process inevitability.
  - Alternatives considered: Defer all cost work to Phase 2 (rejected: contradicts D8 directly; leaves the optimization recommendations economically incomplete through Phase 1; was the prior framing and was wrong). Cost capture at P4 without the per-tenant attribution column (rejected: same retrofit risk as the column-not-built case, just one phase later, and per-tenant attribution is jurisdiction-shaped per D12's logic — cleanest when columns exist from inception of the surface that uses them). Full cost-management surface now including ceilings and routing (rejected: premature in single-model dev environment per D15; the configuration column for ceilings can land later with the enforcement architecture).
  - Kano: must-have.

- **D42: Decision-discipline frameworks — Kano on D-entries, RICE on prioritisation** (Package P3, post-close strategic session)
  - Choice: D-entries that select between alternatives carry a Kano category field at the bottom of the entry: must-have, performance, delighter, indifferent, or reverse. Implementation backlog items and package-level prioritisation when sequencing involves real choice carry RICE scores (Reach, Impact, Confidence, Effort) recorded explicitly. Phase audits include a Kano-distribution check (too many decisions classified as must-have suggests conflation of must-have with default; too many delighters suggests features added without honest weighting) and a RICE-defensibility check (are scores honest forecasts or post-hoc rationalisations). The frameworks are decision-discipline tools, not delivery-throughput tools; they sit alongside D40's DORA-and-CORE4 measurement set, which measures delivery, not decision quality. D-entries from D11 through D40 are not retroactively scored; the convention applies from D41 forward.
  - Reasoning: The case study's audit posture requires that decisions be defensible against external scrutiny. Kano and RICE are widely-recognised product-discipline frameworks; using them visibly on D-entries and prioritisation produces audit artefacts in shapes the target audience already reads. Without explicit categorisation, "must-have" gets stretched to mean "felt-rigorous-while-deciding" and prioritisation arguments lose the structure that distinguishes a forecast from a justification. The frameworks operate at distinct moments — placement in the strategic tree is one moment, option assessment is another, sequencing is a third — and conflating them produces ceremony without reasoning value.
  - Alternatives considered: Frameworks remain informal (rejected: leaves option-assessment and prioritisation reasoning unrecorded, contradicts the audit posture). Kano on every D-entry regardless of whether it selects between alternatives (rejected: forces categorisation on baseline architectural commitments where the choice was foundational, not optional, and the scoring becomes meaningless). RICE on every commitment (rejected: forces forecasting where no real choice was being made between options, producing post-hoc rationalisations).
  - Kano: performance.

- **D43: Living-document discipline for PRDs, package epic notes, and user stories** (Package P3, post-close strategic session)
  - Choice: PRD-shaped documentation surfaces are adopted as living artefacts at phase, package, and session granularity. Phase PRDs live at `charter/phase-N-prd.md`. Package epic notes live at `charter/packages/pN-epic.md`, written at package open and reconciled at package close in the existing package archive at `docs/archive/packages/pN.md` per D31. User stories appear in session prompts using "as [user], I want [capability], so that [outcome]" shape where the framing illuminates rather than constrains. Each artefact is append-only at the version level: the original draft is preserved, the as-built section is appended at audit, and the delta is the explicit deliverable. The convention applies starting P4 for package epic notes; P3's archive at `docs/archive/packages/p3.md` stands as written under the prior convention. The Phase 1 PRD is created retroactively at this strategic session as a v1 baseline against which the Phase 1 close audit measures the as-built reality.
  - Reasoning: Enterprise-procurement audiences read PRD-shaped documentation at evaluation time; the case study's credibility for that audience increases when documentation surfaces appear in shapes the audience expects. Gated upfront PRDs that get edited later freeze intent at the moment of greatest ignorance and lose audit-trail value when subsequent editing erases the original. Living documents with delta capture turn PRD documentation from theatre into evidence of honest learning. The append-only-at-version-level discipline (D29 precedent) is the structural prerequisite that makes living-document delta capture possible at all.
  - Alternatives considered: Gated PRDs with editable history (rejected: editable artefacts lose audit-trail value; contradict the append-only discipline already established in `principles.md`). No PRD-shaped documentation surface (rejected: closes off the documentation shape the target audience reads at evaluation; the case study loses surface area for credibility). Architecture documents as separate artefacts from D-entries (rejected: D-entries already serve the architecture-decision function with stronger alternatives discipline; duplicating the surface produces drift between the two).
  - Kano: must-have.

- **D44: Living roadmap as canonical strategic-tree artefact with versioned reasoning categories** (Package P3, post-close strategic session)
  - Choice: `charter/roadmap.md` is adopted as the canonical living strategic-tree artefact. Bet at the root, initiatives below (phases), epics below that (packages), stories below that (sessions). Versioned, with each version stamped with what changed, why, and the reasoning category drawn from a fixed taxonomy of four: discovery (work surfaced something that changes scope), capacity (operator's available time changes the throughput calculation), signal (data from earlier work changes the priority order), hedge (uncertainty about a future event makes optionality worth more than commitment). Phase audits review the version sequence and the reasoning-category distribution as drift signal: too many capacity-driven changes mean the bet was overscoped; too many signal-driven changes mean the bet was poorly grounded; too many hedge entries mean the operator is avoiding commitment. `charter/packages.md` retained as static reference for direct package list, updated at phase boundaries.
  - Reasoning: Drift-direction is the deliverable of a roadmap, not drift-prevention. A roadmap that never changes is either lucky, lying, or not paying attention. The valuable artefact records course changes with reasoning over time, allowing the audit to distinguish healthy adaptation from directionless drift. Promoting the existing implicit strategic-tree (bet → phase → package → session) to a literal living document with versioned reasoning operationalises the audit posture against the bet's direction.
  - Alternatives considered: Static `packages.md` retained as canonical (rejected: doesn't surface course changes as artefact). Roadmap without reasoning-category taxonomy (rejected: leaves the audit unable to distinguish kinds of drift; "we changed course" is not actionable without "and here is why"). Roadmap with free-form change reasons (rejected: the four-category taxonomy forces honesty about whether a change is discovery or capacity, which is the question that matters; free-form text dodges).
  - Kano: must-have.

- **D45: PRFAQ as living external-voice artefact, refreshed at every phase audit** (Package P3, post-close strategic session)
  - Choice: `charter/prfaq.md` is adopted as a living storytelling artefact in external voice. The press-release section is written in the voice the case study would use to communicate to its target audience (senior product leaders, CPOs, consultancies). The FAQ section is in stakeholder voice. Versioned with reasoning attached to each major rewrite, same shape as roadmap versioning. Audited at every phase boundary on two questions: does the story still hold (would the target audience still care about what's being demonstrated), and does reality still match it (do FAQ answers remain honest given what is built and what has been measured). Standing rule: every phase audit produces a new PRFAQ version, even if changes are small, because the act of re-asking "would the target audience care now?" is itself the discipline.
  - Reasoning: Charter, roadmap, and PRD are written in operator-voice (internal). PRFAQ is the only artefact in external voice. The voice shift forces translation that no internal-voice artefact achieves; press-release voice forces specificity that internal documents allow the operator to dodge. PRFAQ holds a unique role no other charter artefact fills. Drafting now rather than at Phase 2 open produces a baseline against which Phase 2's evolution can be measured, and the act of drafting in external voice during Phase 1 mid-flight surfaces case-study positioning issues earlier than Phase 1 close would.
  - Alternatives considered: PRFAQ deferred to Phase 2 boundary (rejected: misses the storyteller role and forfeits the Phase 1 baseline). PRFAQ as one-shot stress test rather than living artefact (rejected: misses the evolution-as-signal role; the version delta over time is the audit value, not any single version's content).
  - Kano: delighter.

- **D46: Role-function audit categories integrated into phase audits and per-session tagging** (Package P3, post-close strategic session)
  - Choice: Phase audits gain five role-function audit categories: analyst (bet evidence and grounding, market assumptions checked), PM (prioritisation defensibility, RICE-score honesty, scope discipline), architect (D-entries with non-trivial alternatives, principles catching real drift, contract enforcement), engineer (implementation against architectural commitments, test density, mechanical enforcement), technical writer (charter legibility to a non-author reader, narrative coherence across artefacts). Each category is audited against its own quality bar at every phase boundary. Per-session, a one-line role-function tag in the session log entry records which functions were exercised: e.g., `roles: analyst, PM, technical writer` for a strategic session. The distribution of role-function tags over time is signal: many consecutive engineer-only sessions suggest analyst and PM functions are atrophying. Compatible with D40's DORA-and-CORE4 set: those measure delivery throughput; role-function categories measure functional balance and evidence completeness.
  - Reasoning: The case study's proposition is that a senior product leader can direct end-to-end implementation of enterprise-grade software through AI-assisted development; the proposition needs evidence that the product leader's analyst, PM, architect, engineer, and technical-writer functions were all exercised, not just whichever function the operator finds most comfortable. Role-function audit at phase boundaries asks whether each function was done well, regardless of whether the operator or the model produced the artefact. The question is answerable against artefact quality, not against persona styling.
  - Alternatives considered: Single audit category for all work (rejected: different quality bars apply to different functions; conflating produces shallow audits). Persona-styled review (rejected: conflates styling with substance; the question is whether the work meets the bar, not whether the artefact reads in a styled persona's voice).
  - Kano: must-have.

- **D47: Two-surface mode separation maintained by declaration discipline rather than UI separation** (Package P3, post-close strategic session)
  - Choice: Strategic mode and build mode are different work modes, not different UIs. Implementation collapses to a single UI surface (Claude Code as primary, with strategic UI used opportunistically rather than as a load-bearing separation). Mode separation is maintained by four mechanisms: (a) explicit mode declaration at conversation start — "strategic session, deliverable is a charter edit" or "build session for SN, executing the prompt below"; (b) distinct deliverables — strategic conversations produce charter edits, session prompts, or roadmap version updates, build sessions produce code commits and session log entries; (c) distinct commit conventions — `docs(charter): ...` for strategic-mode work that doesn't fit a session number, `docs(pN/sN): ...` and `feat(pN/sN): ...` for build, and `docs(pN/<boundary-name>): ...` for between-packages strategic work; (d) charter files as the bridge between modes unchanged. Reflection-density-by-conversation-type is added to session log expectations: strategic conversations produce shorter entries focused on what was decided, build sessions produce longer entries with substantive reflection on what was learned. The mix of conversation types over time is itself signal at phase audits.
  - Reasoning: The two-UI implementation produced friction without proportionate benefit: charter sync between project-knowledge surfaces and the repo, manual session restart in the strategic UI, window-toggle overhead, and context-switch cost compounded session by session. The mode separation that matters (honest framing, distinct artefacts, distinct cognitive shapes) does not require two UIs; it requires the operator's discipline to mode-switch within whichever UI is active. The case study's whole proposition is that disciplined direction by a product leader produces enterprise-grade systems through AI-assisted development; leaning on operator discipline for mode separation rather than tool separation is consistent with the proposition.
  - Alternatives considered: Two-UI separation maintained (rejected: friction without proportionate benefit; charter sync tax compounds over time). Strategic UI with filesystem connector (rejected: setup overhead, less battle-tested than the build UI's native filesystem access for code-heavy work). Third surface for strategic work (rejected: insufficient evidence about workflow fit; defer until evaluated as a Phase 2 question if friction returns under the single-UI shape).
  - Kano: performance.

- **D48: Mid-session capture surface for stray thoughts** (Package P3, post-close strategic session)
  - Choice: `log/captures.md` is adopted as an append-only mid-session capture surface. Stray thoughts during sessions are written there so they neither derail the current session nor get lost.
