Triage at session close (or at package close for less time-critical captures) classifies each entry into one of five impact types: quick task (small enough to handle this session), inject (insert into the current package's session sequence), defer (forward to the next package or to `deferred-decisions.md`), replan (large enough to warrant rethinking scope; trigger a course-change in the roadmap), note (record only, no action implied). Each entry records the date, originating session id, capture text, triage classification on resolution, and what was done.
  - Reasoning: Without an explicit capture surface, stray-thought management has two failure modes: derailing the current session to chase the thought, or losing the thought to recover focus. Both have shown up across the project's session history. A bounded surface with explicit triage discipline preserves the thought without paying the context-switch cost mid-session. The five-classification taxonomy forces an honest decision at triage time: most captures resolve to "note" or "defer," and the small minority that resolve to "replan" are exactly the captures the project most needs to surface honestly rather than absorb silently.
  - Alternatives considered: Capture in the active session log entry (rejected: pollutes the entry with material that has not been triaged; reduces session-log signal). Capture in `deferred-decisions.md` directly (rejected: skips the triage step; not every capture is a deferred decision). No capture surface (rejected: leaves the operator to choose between derailing and losing the thought, both of which the existing session history shows happening).
  - Kano: performance.

- **D50: TenantContext value object — shape, location, and propagation** (Package P4, Session 15)
  - Choice: A request-scoped tenant identity payload lands as ``shared_kernel/tenant_context.py`` carrying three immutable fields: ``tenant_id`` (UUID-shaped str), ``jurisdiction`` (str), and ``cost_attribution_id`` (str). Shape: stdlib ``dataclass(frozen=True)`` with ``__post_init__`` validation that rejects empty strings on each field. Location: ``shared_kernel/`` rather than a context-specific module, because the value object must be referentially equal across contexts (the inference adapter consumes it; future audit, observability, and orchestration paths will too) per D16's shared-kernel policy. Propagation pattern: hybrid — FastAPI ``Depends(get_tenant_context)`` resolves the context from the control-plane registry at the router boundary, then the resolved value flows as an explicit parameter through the application use case to the adapter. The router's resolver calls ``registry.get_tenant`` against the principal's UUID-shaped tenant_id, returning HTTP 404 if absent and HTTP 400 if the principal carries a non-UUID sentinel (operator-context tokens, which are not registry rows). The ``Tenant`` aggregate gains a ``cost_attribution_id`` field (read by ``_row_to_tenant`` from the column landed at S14, populated by ``register_tenant`` from ``str(tenant_id)`` per D41's 1:1 mapping at inception). Tests substitute via FastAPI's ``app.dependency_overrides[get_tenant_context]``. The ``InferencePort`` signature widens from ``tenant_id: TenantId`` to ``tenant_context: TenantContext``; the LiteLLMAdapter span emits three Padhanam-domain ``tenant.*`` attributes (``tenant.id``, ``tenant.jurisdiction``, ``tenant.cost_attribution_id``) extending the namespace D37 established. The legacy ``padhanam.tenant_id`` attribute from S7 is removed in the same commit; ``tenant.id`` is the single source. Classification field deferred per S15 framing decision option C — no ``classification`` column exists on ``tenant_registry`` and no tenant-configuration table exists at P4. Classification lands at whichever package genuinely consumes it (P7 or P8 per ``charter/packages/p4-epic.md`` out-of-scope).
  - Reasoning: D27 commits OTel as the portability boundary; the Padhanam-domain ``tenant.*`` namespace inherits the same forward-compat property D37 and D49 established (rename is local to the adapter span if OTel converges on a different multi-tenant namespace). D32/D33 commit per-tenant database isolation; the TenantContext is the request-scoped projection that the adapter span emits without re-reading the registry, making jurisdiction available for future routing decisions and ``cost_attribution_id`` available at the trace level for the recommendation engine D9 commits. D35's per-tenant audit chain independence is preserved: the inference path emits no audit rows, and the integration test asserts no cross-tenant write occurs against either data plane. D41 commits cost capture and per-tenant attribution; the ``cost_attribution_id`` field carries the per-tenant cost-attribution column from the registry through to the trace, which is the audit-shaped surface P11's optimization recommendations will consume. D49's cost-attribute wiring shape inherits cleanly: the four ``gen_ai.cost.*`` attributes co-exist on the same span as the three ``tenant.*`` attributes, with no namespace collision. The shape choice (frozen dataclass) is forced by the import-linter ``shared-kernel-policed`` contract that forbids Pydantic in shared_kernel/ per D16; ``Tenant``'s ``frozen=True`` precedent makes the dataclass shape consistent with the rest of the domain. Propagation pattern (hybrid Depends + parameter passing) follows the existing precedent of the inference router's ``get_principal`` and ``get_inference_port`` Depends, then explicit parameters through the use case → port → adapter chain; ContextVar would invert the explicit-passing pattern, and pure Depends through internal layers would couple them to FastAPI. The classification deferral surfaces a methodology observation worth surfacing: field commitments at framing should be verified against ``charter/schema.md`` before close, because the v1 epic note named ``classification`` as a TenantContext field while no column exists.
  - Alternatives considered: Pydantic v2 model with ``frozen=True`` for the value object (rejected: ``shared-kernel-policed`` import-linter contract forbids Pydantic in shared_kernel/; the dataclass shape is the only option compliant with D16). Location in a context-specific module under ``contexts/tenancy/domain/`` (rejected: TenantContext is consumed by the inference adapter at minimum and will be consumed by audit, observability, and orchestration paths in later packages; locating in tenancy/ would force cross-context imports through the api facade for what is essentially a typing primitive, contrary to the shared_kernel role). Propagation via ``contextvars.ContextVar`` set in middleware and read in adapter (rejected: inverts the explicit-passing pattern that the inference path follows from S7; hides the dependency at the type-signature level and produces test-fragility around context isolation in async code). Pure FastAPI ``Depends`` injection through internal layers (rejected: couples the use case and adapter to FastAPI, which contradicts the hexagonal commitment that the application and ports layers stay framework-free). Adding a ``classification`` field to TenantContext for forward-affordance (rejected: no schema column exists, no consumer reads it, the field would be meaningless until P7/P8 consumes it; adding now is paper architecture per the same principle that rejected committing OTel cost-attribute namespaces ahead of stabilisation). Keeping ``tenant.cost_attribution_id`` internal to the adapter rather than emitting as a span attribute (rejected: the audit-shaped surface for cost rollup that P11 will consume needs the field at the trace level per D41's rationale; making it observable rather than internal is the consistent extension of D37's tenant.* namespace). New ``contexts.tenancy.api.resolve_tenant_context`` method returning a TenantContext directly rather than extending ``Tenant`` with cost_attribution_id (rejected: adding a parallel resolution path duplicates the registry-read surface; extending ``Tenant`` to match the schema is structurally cleaner and the registry adapter already reads the row).
  - Kano: performance.

- **D49: Cost-capture wiring shape** (Package P4, Session 14)
  - Choice: D41's cost-capture commitment lands as three integration points wired together at S14. (1) Pricing table at `padhanam/config/inference.py`: a separate `ModelPricing` Pydantic model and a module-level `PRICING_TABLE` dict alongside `InferenceSettings`, plus a `cost_for(model, input_tokens, output_tokens)` helper returning a frozen `CostBreakdown`. The table covers `qwen2.5:7b` at zero rates (honest for the Ollama-hosted dev model) and `gpt-4o-mini` at the published rates 0.150/0.600 USD per 1M input/output tokens; the monthly review cadence in `ops/scheduled_checks.yaml` (D41) catches drift. (2) OTel attribute emission on the LiteLLMAdapter span: four attributes per completion, `gen_ai.cost.input_usd`, `gen_ai.cost.output_usd`, `gen_ai.cost.total_usd`, and `gen_ai.cost.pricing_status` (values `table_hit` and `unknown_model`). The cost attributes are Padhanam-domain rather than borrowed from the OTel GenAI semantic-conventions group, which has not stabilised cost-attribute naming as of 2026-05-05; the namespace choice follows D27 (OTel as the portability boundary; specific attribute names are domain choices that travel through OTel cleanly) and matches the precedent D37 set under the same not-stabilised condition for `tenant.id` and `tenant.jurisdiction`. If OTel converges on (e.g.) `gen_ai.usage.cost.*`, the migration is a span-attribute rename plus a one-line update at consumers. (3) Cost computation: own implementation against `PRICING_TABLE`, not `litellm.completion_cost` or the response object's `_hidden_params.response_cost`. The vendor helper reads from LiteLLM's bundled `model_prices_and_context_window` dictionary, which couples Padhanam's cost numbers to LiteLLM's release cadence and bundled pricing data; the dev/Ollama zero-cost case is also not clean against the bundled dictionary. Unknown-model lookups return zeros plus the `unknown_model` pricing-status flag rather than breaking inference; observability can alert on the flag and the monthly review reconciles it.
  - Reasoning: D9 (recommendation-shape requirement) needs cost attached at the trace level so the optimization layer at P11 can produce the third recommendation shape ("this costs N% more for M% quality at the same task type"); D8 commits cost-per-successful-task as a Phase 1 metric, so the wiring work to mechanically realise it was already in scope; D41 commits per-tenant cost attribution as constitutive to the case study's audit posture. D27 commits OTel as the portability boundary, which is what makes a Padhanam-domain attribute namespace honest rather than vendor-locked: the attributes flow through OTel cleanly, and the namespace is adjustable as conventions stabilise. Pricing-table format choice (b) over (a) keeps pricing as pure code data separate from settings — co-locating it on `InferenceSettings` would force callers to construct settings (with the required `litellm_master_key`) just to read pricing, which is an awkward coupling between configuration-environment data and configuration-as-code data. Format (c) (YAML/TOML under `ops/`) is deferred until pricing complexity (multi-region rates, time-zoned rates, large rate cards) justifies the extra load surface. Cost-helper sourcing — own implementation versus LiteLLM's helper — favours the helper if the bundled pricing data covers the full set of routed models honestly; with `qwen2.5:7b` at zero (Ollama-hosted, no per-call vendor cost) outside that data, and the case study committing to a monthly review surface that lives at the platform layer rather than at the vendor's release cadence, the coupling cost of using the vendor helper exceeds the implementation cost of `cost_for()`. Cost-ceiling forward-affordance columns landing alongside the cost-attribution column is the same retrofit-avoidance logic D41 used for the cost-attribution column itself; the columns are constrained by CHECK at the schema layer (action space pinned to `block`, `throttle`, `notify`, `audit_only`) so values cannot land before enforcement consumes them, but no code path reads them at S14.
  - Alternatives considered: Use `litellm.completion_cost` (rejected: couples Padhanam's cost numbers to LiteLLM's bundled pricing dictionary and release cadence; the dev/Ollama zero-cost case is not clean against the bundled pricing). Pricing table as a typed field on `InferenceSettings` — option (a) (rejected: pricing is pure code data, not environment-driven; tests/callers should not need to construct a Settings instance with the required master key just to read pricing). Pricing table as YAML/TOML under `ops/` — option (c) (deferred: file-IO load surface is wasted complexity at the current single-region, single-table-row pricing scale; revisit at the structural threshold when multi-region or rate-card complexity arrives). Adopt OTel's working-draft cost-attribute namespace (rejected: the working draft has not stabilised as of 2026-05-05 and committing now would force a rename when it does converge; D27 makes the rename cheap so deferring the namespace decision until OTel stabilises is honest). Hard-fail inference on unknown-model pricing lookups (rejected: a model routed through LiteLLM but missing from the pricing table is configuration drift, not a user-request failure; the `pricing_status="unknown_model"` attribute plus the monthly review surfaces drift without breaking calls). Defer cost-ceiling forward-affordance columns to a later session (rejected: avoidable retrofit per D41's reasoning; the columns are cheap to add at S14 versus expensive to migrate later). Make cost-ceiling columns enum types via Postgres `CREATE TYPE` (rejected: text + CHECK is migration-friendlier than enum types, which require `ALTER TYPE ADD VALUE` with subtle transaction restrictions when the action space evolves).
  - Kano: must-have.

- **D51: PRFAQ voice and audience supersede D45's case-study framing** (Package P4-post, carryover-cleanup strategic session)
  - Choice: `charter/prfaq.md` v2 onward uses vendor product-launch voice in the press-release section and vendor voice in the FAQ section, with AI Labs teams and procurement as the target audience. The press release announces platform launch in the form a procurement reader recognises as the standard PRFAQ shape (vendor announces platform, named target customer quote, named executive quote, supporting product detail). The FAQ answers procurement-shaped questions in vendor voice. The case-study framing remains the proposition (per D39 and `bet.md`); the case-study evidence lives in `bet.md`, `charter/methodology.md`, and the public commit history, not in the PRFAQ. PRFAQ remains a living artefact refreshed at every phase audit per D45's living-artefact commitment, which D51 preserves intact — only the voice and audience are superseded; the artefact's living-document discipline, append-only version log, and phase-audit refresh cadence are unchanged.
  - Reasoning: D45's case-study voice misjudged the artefact's structural form. Amazon-style PRFAQ is fundamentally a vendor product-launch artefact; forcing case-study voice onto it produced v1's `[OPERATOR REWRITE — case-study positioning]` and `[OPERATOR REWRITE — stakeholder voice]` markers throughout the press release and FAQ, which were canary signals that something deeper than voice was wrong. The structural-form mismatch surfaced at v1 implementation: the markers indicated awkwardness at every rewrite candidate. The case-study evidence lives elsewhere in the charter (the bet document is the strategic articulation; the methodology document is the architect-implementer-pattern artefact; the public commit history is the record); PRFAQ goes back to its proper shape and stops doing case-study work it is not the right artefact for. The audience shift to AI Labs teams and procurement reflects who actually reads PRFAQ-shaped artefacts in vendor voice at the moment of evaluation; the case study's eventual L&D and advisory audience reads `bet.md`, `methodology.md`, and the commit history instead. The voice and audience pair are coupled: vendor voice without procurement audience reads as marketing copy; procurement audience without vendor voice reads as a research write-up. Both have to land together for the artefact to do the work it is for.
  - Alternatives considered: Preserve D45's case-study voice and continue rewriting v1 to fit (rejected: the structural-form mismatch surfaces every rewrite as awkward; the markers are signal, not noise; the operator-rewrite work expands rather than converges under this choice). Defer PRFAQ to Phase 2 framing (rejected: forfeits the Phase 1 baseline that D45 committed and the act of writing in external voice during Phase 1 mid-flight per D45's reasoning; the discipline of refreshing at every phase audit needs a v1-to-v2 baseline to measure against, and Phase 1 close audit would land without one). Multiple PRFAQ documents for different audiences — case-study voice for L&D readers, vendor voice for procurement readers (rejected: the artefact is one document refreshed at phase audits per D45; multiplying surfaces dilutes the discipline and produces two version logs that would inevitably drift apart). Vendor voice with broader audience (e.g., "any enterprise buying agentic systems") (rejected: the AI Labs teams + procurement audience is specific enough that the press-release detail and FAQ questions can be concrete rather than generic; broader audiences produce vaguer artefacts).
  - Kano: must-have.

- **D52: Identity foundation deferred to Phase 2, supersession of D3** (Package P4-post, carryover-cleanup strategic session)
  - Choice: Identity foundation (Keycloak realm, OIDC integration, SAML SP, SCIM 2.0 endpoint, federated session management) is deferred from Phase 1 to Phase 2 in explicit supersession of D3. Phase 1 retains auth-middleware-on-every-endpoint per D23 with the dev signed-token backend and the production-shaped Keycloak backend stubbed; this is sufficient evidence that the architecture is identity-ready. Phase 2 activates real federated identity when production deployment context arrives — real IdP connections, SCIM provisioning from real HR systems, SAML against enterprise IdPs. D10's Keycloak 26 stack-version pin stands for Phase 2 activation; the version pin is preserved (not deleted) since Phase 2 still commits to Keycloak as the reference IdP.
  - Reasoning: The bet's load-bearing claims do not turn on federated identity. Phase 1's substrate proves architectural discipline survives AI-assisted implementation, mechanical enforcement scales, and observability with the cost dimension produces optimisation recommendations enterprise procurement reads as defensible. None of those claims exercises Keycloak, SAML, OIDC, or SCIM. Running Keycloak in dev Compose against mocked enterprise IdPs would demonstrate Keycloak operates correctly, not that the platform's federated-identity story works against real enterprise IdPs; that demonstration requires production deployment context Phase 1 does not have. D3's original commitment was structural; superseding it requires explicit accounting in the decision log, not silent drift. The drift was uncaught from S4 forward and surfaced 2026-05-06 by a status-snapshot pass on a quiet between-packages day; the failure-modes entry in `charter/methodology.md` records the discipline addition (charter-vs-archive consistency check at every package close) that prevents recurrence. The audit posture is honest only if the catch mechanism is named honestly: the catch was operator-driven analysis, not mechanical enforcement, and the methodology document records the gap rather than claiming the existing audit posture would have caught this.
  - Alternatives considered: Ship identity foundation as a thirteenth Phase 1 package (rejected: substantial scope expansion late in Phase 1; identity is procurement table-stakes but not what the proposition turns on; the bet's load-bearing claims are evidenced without it; capacity-side cost would compress P5–P12 against the Phase 1 close audit timing). Absorb identity into an existing Phase 1 package (rejected: no remaining Phase 1 package has natural identity scope, and bolting it onto an unrelated package would create the same procurement-realism gap as silent deferral while breaking package coherence; the proper home is a dedicated Phase 2 package). Continue silent deferral (rejected: that is precisely the drift this D-entry corrects; pretending identity is in Phase 1 when nothing is built and nothing is on deck is dishonest about the actual roadmap and erodes the case study's audit posture). Deprecate D3 entirely without Phase 2 commitment (rejected: federated identity is procurement-required for any real enterprise tenant at production; deferring is honest, deprecating without commitment understates the architectural commitment Padhanam intends to honour).
  - Kano: must-have.

- **D53: P5 evaluation harness framing — scoring sheet primitive, per-tenant storage, appliers-as-data, Reading-C human-oversight posture** (Package P5, P5-open strategic block, 2026-05-06)
  - Choice: P5 ships the evaluation harness as a bounded context at `contexts/evaluation/` per D16. The evaluation primitive is the scoring sheet (the user-facing name for what the eval literature calls a rubric): a named record carrying criteria, levels per criterion, and definitions; versioned and immutable per version, with `created_by_user_id` and `created_at` on every revision; role-gated authorship enforced at the inbound adapter (Phase 1: tenant-operator role via D23's signed-token backend; Phase 2: real Keycloak roles per D52). Rubric-application records reference the revision id, not the sheet id, so historical evaluations stay anchored to the version they were applied against. Storage is per-tenant DB only; the platform-baseline scoring sheet library is deferred to a later activation condition (real onboarding flow per D13, or a cross-tenant curated library with at least one real consumer beyond demoware). Each criterion is applied by one of three applier modes: deterministic (a Python function in a small bounded library inside `contexts/evaluation/`), prompt (LLM-as-judge, stored as a data record carrying the judge prompt template, subject to the same authorship and versioning model as the scoring sheet itself), or human (a reviewer applies the criterion directly; UI deferred per the Reading-C posture below). Rubric-application records carry `automated_score`, `human_score`, `reviewed_by_user_id`, and `confirmed_at` from inception, so the substrate for calibration learning and the audit trail for human review both exist before the human-review UI ships. Reading-C posture: the architecture absorbs human review at the data-model layer; P5 ships only the automated spine; UI for human review is named explicitly out-of-scope and deferred to P10 (audit-log viewer) or P11 (optimization dashboard) territory.
  - Reasoning: D8 commits cost-per-successful-task as a Phase 1 metric; D9 commits recommendation-shaped output requiring calibrated evaluation data; D41 wires cost into the trace level at P4; P5's harness consumes those into the metric and produces the data the P11 recommendation engine reads. The case study's audit posture requires that automated evaluation be an accelerator, not a substitute, for human oversight; AI-Labs-grade reviewers do not trust automated-only eval pipelines for high-stakes outputs. Reading C lands that posture at the data-model layer without pulling UI scope into a CLI-shaped package. Per-tenant storage falls cleanly inside D1 (database-per-tenant) and D32 (no shared tables for tenant data); putting scoring sheets in the control-plane would require either weakening D32 or adopting operator-only-authorship semantics, which is the platform-baseline case (deferred). "Scoring sheet" over "rubric" as the user-facing name reduces pedagogical baggage without changing the underlying primitive (the rubric-application record name stays, since it is the term-of-art for the stored result). Versioning and immutability mirrors D32/D37 history-shape (without hash-chain, which is tamper-evidence-shaped and not load-bearing for scoring sheets). Appliers-as-data for prompt appliers respects the change-velocity asymmetry: judge prompts iterate fast and need to live in the same versioning and authorization model as the scoring sheet itself; treating them as code would force every prompt tweak through a deploy event, defeating the iterate-fast nature of LLM-as-judge tuning. Deterministic primitives stay as code because the deterministic applier set is small and stable; new ones are rare; the change-velocity asymmetry does not bite.
  - Alternatives considered: Reading A (automated-first, human review entirely deferred; rejected: leaves the case-study posture undefended, AI-Labs-grade reviewers refuse to trust automated-only pipelines for high-stakes evaluation). Reading B (rubric-first with human-review UI as P5 spine; rejected: pulls UI scope into a CLI-shaped package, conflicts with the roadmap framing of P5, premature given the absence of a multi-reviewer Phase 1 use case). Hybrid storage with control-plane platform-baseline library at P5 (rejected: no Phase-1 forcing function, premature scope per the D11/D52 narrow-scope discipline; the migration path stays mechanical when activation arrives because the schema is identical, so deferring costs nothing). "Rubric" as the user-facing name (rejected: pedagogical baggage; "scoring sheet" is layman-readable without changing the primitive). Appliers all as code (rejected: forces judge-prompt tweaks through deploy events). Appliers all as data including deterministic primitives (rejected: adds an evaluation-engine indirection layer for primitives that change rarely, paper architecture). Filesystem storage for scoring sheets (rejected: breaks D1/D32 DB-shaped tenant isolation discipline). Hash-chained scoring sheet revisions (rejected: tamper-evidence is not load-bearing for scoring sheets; history-shaped versioning is sufficient).
  - Kano: must-have.

- **D54: Applier port shape — single polymorphic async ApplierPort with applier_type dispatch in the adapter** (Package P5, Session 16, 2026-05-06)
  - Choice: ``ApplierPort`` is a single async Protocol with the signature ``async def apply(*, interaction: Interaction, output: str, criterion: Criterion, applier_config: ApplierConfig) -> str | None``. A single polymorphic adapter (``ExactMatchApplier`` at ``contexts/evaluation/adapters/outbound/exact_match_applier.py``) implements the port and dispatches on ``applier_config.applier_type``: deterministic resolves the function name against the deterministic-library registry at ``contexts/evaluation/adapters/outbound/deterministic_library.py`` and invokes it; prompt and human raise ``NotImplementedError`` at S16 (prompt lands at S17 LLM-as-judge per D53; human is data-substrate-only per D53's Reading-C posture and never invokes the port). The use case at ``contexts/evaluation/application/apply_scoring_sheet.py`` is indifferent to applier mechanism — it asks "score this output against this criterion," receives a score string, and constructs the ``RubricApplication`` from that plus the context it already holds (interaction, criterion, applier id, revision id). Build-time deviation from S16 framing: the framing's recommended return type was ``RubricApplication``; we landed ``str | None`` because the applier should not own record assembly (the persistence-layer fields ``id`` and ``created_at``, plus context fields like ``interaction_id`` the applier has no opinion about, leak into the port if the port assembles the record). The use case is the structural authority on RubricApplication construction; the applier is the structural authority on scoring. The honest split lives at the score type, not at the record type.
  - Reasoning: D53 frames appliers-as-data; the use case is indifferent to applier mechanism, so a single polymorphic port matches that indifference. Two typed sub-ports (``DeterministicApplierPort`` sync + ``PromptApplierPort`` async) would push dispatch knowledge up the stack — the use case would route on ``applier_type`` itself, which is the kind of detail the port exists to hide. Async at the port level is forward-compatible with the LLM-as-judge applier landing at S17. The build experience supports the choice: at one concrete deterministic adapter (S16's ``exact_match``), the dispatch logic in ``ExactMatchApplier.apply`` stayed mechanical — a simple ``if/elif`` over the three ``ApplierType`` values, with the deterministic branch resolving against the small registry and the other branches raising. No domain-shaped knowledge leaked into the dispatch; the choice of executable behaviour was data, exactly as D53 framed it. Dispatch-mechanical contingency carried over from framing: if at S17's prompt-applier addition the switch logic begins encoding domain knowledge (e.g. needing criterion shape to pick a function, or needing tenant context to pick a judge model), this commits revisits at S17 framing. At S16 the contingency does not bite. Return-shape choice (``str | None`` rather than ``RubricApplication``) is the structurally honest split between scoring (the applier's responsibility) and record assembly (the use case's responsibility); ``None`` is reserved for the human-applier mode that never invokes the port at S16 but may return null automated_score in future flows where the applier explicitly defers to human review.
  - Alternatives considered: Two typed sub-ports — DeterministicApplierPort (sync) + PromptApplierPort (async) (rejected: pushes dispatch into the use case for no test-level benefit; the use case's indifference to applier mechanism is a feature of the appliers-as-data framing, and routing on applier_type at the use-case layer would invert that). Single sync port with the adapter wrapping LLM calls in run_in_executor (rejected: blocking adapter pattern is anti-FastAPI-async; LLM calls dominate runtime at S17 onward and forcing them through a sync-bridge is paper architecture). Return type ``RubricApplication`` per the framing prompt (rejected at build per the reasoning above; the structural split between scoring and record assembly is cleaner at the score type, and the port stays free of persistence-layer concerns the applier does not own). Adapter-per-applier-type (one adapter for deterministic, one for prompt, one for human; rejected: the use case would need to know which adapter to dispatch to, which is the appliers-as-data framing inverted; a single polymorphic adapter with internal dispatch is the structurally honest shape for "the executable behaviour is data the adapter reads"). Static dispatch on applier_type via match statement instead of if/elif (acceptable shape variant; current if/elif chosen for trivial readability difference at three values).
  - Kano: performance.

- **D55: Score representation on rubric_applications — text with criterion-level interpretation** (Package P5, Session 16, 2026-05-06)
  - Choice: ``automated_score`` and ``human_score`` on ``rubric_applications`` are TEXT columns. Score interpretation is criterion-level: each criterion's ``levels`` jsonb encodes the level definitions (label-plus-definition pairs), which downstream consumers (cost-per-successful-task at S17, regression report at S18, recommendation surface at P11) read to determine pass/fail, threshold breaches, or aggregations. SQL aggregation across rubric_applications requires explicit casting plus criterion-level filtering; direct ``AVG(automated_score)`` is foreclosed and is not the intended access pattern. The criterion is the architectural authority on what its scores mean, and the harness's downstream metric implementations read the criterion's level definitions to determine "successful," "threshold breach," or "aggregation method" rather than relying on a generic numeric default.
  - Reasoning: Rubric heterogeneity is real. Production scoring sheets use numeric scales (1–5 Likert), categorical labels (pass/fail, yes/no/partial), and continuous similarity scores (0.0–1.0). The text representation preserves all three shapes at one column cost — no schema split, no NULL columns for scoring shapes a particular criterion does not use, no implicit numeric default that pretends pass/fail rubrics are just "1 or 0." The foreclosure of direct SQL aggregation is the trade-off accepted: criterion-level interpretation lives where the criterion is defined, which is the right architectural authority on what its scores mean. S17's cost-per-successful-task implementation reads the criterion's level definitions to determine "successful," and S18's regression report reads them to determine threshold breaches; this is more honest than a numeric default that pretends every rubric is comparable on a uniform scale. Locking the choice in at S16 prevents a quality-foreclosing default leaking into the schema; superseding D55 later would require a migration on every rubric_applications row, which is the kind of avoidable retrofit D41 named explicitly as a learning failure when the case study commits to its audit posture.
  - Alternatives considered: Numeric column plus text label (rejected: two columns where one suffices when criterion encodes interpretation; categorical-only criteria still need the text path; the column split implies a runtime split that is paper architecture). Jsonb discriminated type (e.g. ``{"kind": "numeric", "value": 4}`` vs ``{"kind": "categorical", "value": "pass"}``) (rejected: opaque to SQL beyond extraction, query cost compounds at the optimization-recommendation layer, flexibility unused at any concrete criterion shape since each criterion already declares its level shape via ``levels``). Numeric only (rejected: forecloses categorical and binary scoring without ad-hoc encoding; pass/fail rubrics are common in procurement-grade evaluation where the binary outcome is exactly what matters). Numeric with criterion-level scaling map at write time (rejected: pushes coercion into criterion authoring; criterion writers should describe levels with definitions, not encode them onto a numeric scale chosen for SQL convenience). Enum types via Postgres ``CREATE TYPE`` (rejected: cross-criterion enum sharing forces every criterion's level set into a single global enum, which collapses the criterion-as-authority framing; per-criterion enum types proliferate type definitions without benefit).
  - Kano: must-have. The choice constrains every downstream consumer of rubric_applications; locking in at S16 prevents a quality-foreclosing default leaking into the schema, and the foreclosure of direct SQL aggregation is the structural trade-off the case study commits to in exchange for honest cross-rubric scoring.

- **D56: TraceQueryPort method shape — interface segregation, separate methods per access pattern** (Package P5, Session S17b, 2026-05-06)
  - Choice: TraceQueryPort has two methods, not one widened method. ``get_trace(trace_id, tenant_context) -> TraceRecord | None`` retains its S7-era shape (now widened to TenantContext per sub-D50 application) for rich full-trace fetch by the recommendation engine's later use. ``get_costs_by_trace_ids(trace_ids, tenant_context) -> dict[str, CostBreakdown]`` is the new batch cost-only method serving cost-per-successful-task and aggregate cost queries. Each method serves a coherent access pattern; neither over-generalised. The third method, ``list_recent_traces``, is also widened to TenantContext for sub-D50 consistency but otherwise unchanged.
  - Reasoning: Widening get_trace to a batch form forces cost-shaped consumers to filter span data they don't need, which compounds at the recommendation engine layer where the same TraceQueryPort is consumed for different access patterns. Collapsing to a generic ``get_traces(ids, fields)`` loses type safety and pushes field-selection complexity into every consumer. Interface segregation on ports is the principle: each method serves an access pattern with type-safe inputs and outputs. As observability access patterns multiply (recommendation engine adding aggregation queries at P11; active testing surface at P12), the pattern is to add methods, not widen methods. D17's facade pattern at the api.py level handles the public-surface stability concern; the port itself can grow methods without breaking consumers because each consumer depends on the methods it calls, not the entire port surface.
  - Alternatives considered: Widen get_trace to batch form (rejected: forces cost consumers to filter span data they do not need; access-pattern shape leaks across consumers; the recommendation engine and the cost-rollup have legitimately different needs). Generic ``get_traces(ids, fields)`` with field selection (rejected: loses type safety; pushes field-selection logic into every consumer; over-engineered for current scale and the consumer count). Single method serving both with optional field filter (rejected: same losses as the generic shape; complicates the implementation without simplifying the consumer side).
  - Kano: performance.

- **D57: Cost-query path — two-layer abstraction (evaluation → observability use case → trace store port)** (Package P5, Session S17b, 2026-05-06)
  - Choice: ``contexts/evaluation/`` does not query Langfuse directly. The cost-query path is two layers: (1) ``contexts/observability/`` owns the trace-store abstraction via ``TraceQueryPort`` and the ``LangfuseHTTPTraceQueryAdapter``; (2) ``contexts/observability/application/query_cost_by_trace_ids`` exposes a use case that other contexts call via the ``contexts.observability.api`` facade. ``contexts/evaluation/`` defines its own ``CostQueryPort`` and an adapter that calls ``contexts.observability.api.query_cost_by_trace_ids``. Two layers; each layer owns its concern. The import-linter contracts gain ``ignore_imports`` clauses on each context's api.py → its own application/domain edges so the api facade pattern is allowed structurally rather than working in spite of the contracts.
  - Reasoning: D27 forbids vendor-specific code outside dedicated adapters. A direct Langfuse query in ``contexts/evaluation/application/`` would put Langfuse-specific knowledge in a domain context that has no business knowing about Langfuse; the case study's posture treats this as paper architecture that compounds with every additional cost consumer. The two-layer abstraction puts vendor knowledge in observability adapters (where D27 wants it) and exposes a vendor-agnostic interface to consumers (evaluation today, P11 recommendation engine tomorrow). The pattern is the structural precedent for every future cross-context cost or trace consumer: the consumer defines its own port and adapter, and the adapter calls the source context's api facade. D17's facade pattern provides the import target; D57 commits to the access pattern that makes the facade load-bearing rather than ornamental. The import-linter refinement makes D17's "the .api module is the public surface" honest at the contract level; the existing independence contracts were stricter than D17 actually warrants and the api-mediated cross-context flow had to be explicitly enabled to land cleanly.
  - Alternatives considered: Direct Langfuse query in evaluation (rejected: D27 violation; vendor knowledge leaks into evaluation domain; compounds across consumers). Single layer — evaluation imports observability's TraceQueryPort directly without an evaluation-side port (rejected: bypasses the application-layer use case; couples evaluation to observability's port surface, which is observability's internal concern, not its public contract). Trace data flowing through OTel pipeline into evaluation's own storage (rejected: paper architecture at Phase 1 scale; adds infrastructure complexity for a query that is a single HTTP fetch). CostQueryPort method that takes ``CostBreakdown`` from evaluation's own domain rather than via the observability api facade (rejected: duplicates the value object across contexts; observability is the architectural authority on what trace cost means).
  - Kano: must-have.

- **D58: Regression report shape — single-baseline comparison, per-criterion success-rate deltas, aggregate-only cost-per-task, text and JSON output** (Package P5, Session S18, 2026-05-06)
  - Choice: The regression report compares two runs (baseline revision vs candidate revision) of a scoring sheet against the same interaction set. ``RegressionReport`` carries ``baseline_revision_id``, ``candidate_revision_id``, ``interaction_set_id``, a tuple of per-criterion deltas, aggregate metrics, and a ``generated_at`` timestamp. ``CriterionDelta`` carries ``criterion_name`` (the join key across revisions; criterion ids differ because each revision creates fresh criterion rows), per-side success rates, the rate delta, and per-side application counts so consumers can distinguish "no data" from "all failures". ``AggregateMetrics`` carries the overall counts, success-rate, and cost-per-task on each side plus the cost-per-task delta. Output renders in two formats: human-readable markdown text (the operator's terminal surface) and structured JSON (the P11 recommendation engine's evidence input). Cost-per-task lives at the aggregate level only; per-criterion cost is P11 territory because trace_ids are shared across criteria scoring the same model output, making cost structurally per-trace rather than per-criterion. Single-baseline at S18; multi-baseline regression tracking (run history with trend lines) defers to P11 territory or beyond. Build-time refinements from the S18 brief, both consistent with the framing-prompt-as-recommendation methodology pattern (D54 / S15 / S16): brief named ``baseline_run_id`` / ``candidate_run_id`` and per-criterion cost fields on ``CriterionDelta``; refined to ``baseline_revision_id`` / ``candidate_revision_id`` (UUID, the user-facing handle the data model exposes — there is no Run entity) and dropped per-criterion cost from ``CriterionDelta`` (the existing ``cost_per_successful_task`` use case returns aggregate-only data; per-criterion cost adds query work for a P11-shaped consumer that does not exist at S18).
  - Reasoning: The eval harness's user-facing question is "is the candidate better than the baseline?" — single-baseline comparison is the minimal answer, and the per-criterion + aggregate split serves both detail-oriented (which criterion regressed) and headline-oriented (overall delta) consumers. Joining criteria across revisions by ``criterion.name`` reflects that the human-stable identity of a criterion is its name, not its UUID (each revision creates fresh criterion rows per D53's immutability commitment, so ids never match across revisions). The asymmetric-criteria handling — a criterion that exists only in baseline or only in candidate produces a ``CriterionDelta`` with zero on the missing side and the per-side counts to interpret it — is honest about the structural absence rather than fabricating a delta that hides the renaming. Text output serves the operator running CLI evaluation (the markdown table is paste-ready into a PR description, a Slack message, or a session log entry); JSON output serves programmatic consumers (the P11 recommendation engine reads regression reports as evidence). Decimal cost values render as strings in JSON because ``json`` has no Decimal type and the float fallback would surrender the monetary precision D49 commits to keeping. Multi-baseline tracking (e.g., "this candidate vs the last 10 baselines") is a P11+ concern that requires run-history infrastructure beyond P5's scope; landing it now is paper architecture against an absent consumer. The single-baseline shape is forward-compatible: a multi-baseline report is a list of single-baseline reports plus aggregation, so the data model extends without restructuring. Build-time field-name and field-set refinements from the brief land per the methodology's framing-prompt-as-recommendation pattern: brief specifics name an option space and the strongest recommendation; build commits to the choice with whatever refinements the actual code surfaces; the D-entry records both. Same shape as D54's return-type deviation (``str | None`` vs the brief's ``RubricApplication``), S15's commit-shape granularity adjustment, and S17b's ``>= 0`` assertion adjustment — fourth instance of the pattern across P5.
  - Alternatives considered: Multi-baseline with trend tracking (rejected: paper architecture at S18; P11 territory; absent consumer; adding now would pre-commit to a run-history shape before P9 ships the run-history infrastructure). Per-trace report (rejected: too granular for the harness's user-facing question; trace-level inspection lives in the optimization dashboard at P11). Per-criterion cost on ``CriterionDelta`` (rejected at build per the brief's own out-of-scope statement and the structural reality that trace_ids are shared across criteria; promoting per-criterion cost would force the use case to query costs once per criterion's trace_id set, which is N criteria × the same cost-query cost without surfacing meaningfully different data). JSON-only output (rejected: text rendering for the CLI operator is load-bearing for case-study posture; the harness should be operable without programmatic tooling). Text-only output (rejected: programmatic consumers at P11 need the structured shape; rendering JSON from text is parse-error-prone). Joining criteria by id rather than name across revisions (rejected: ids change across revisions per D53's immutability commitment; the join would always produce zero per-criterion deltas with both ids present in the union, making the report useless). Float for cost in JSON output (rejected: surrenders the monetary precision D49 went out of its way to keep; Decimal-as-string is the conventional JSON-compatibility shape for monetary values). Run-id as a free-form string carried alongside revision ids (rejected: introduces a synonym for the same handle; the data model has no Run entity, and the closest concept — the (revision, interaction_set, tenant) tuple — is already represented in the ids the report carries).
  - Kano: must-have for P5 close. The regression report is the harness's user-facing surface and the substrate the P11 recommendation engine reads as evidence; without a documented shape, every consumer reinvents the comparison and the P11 recommendation surface cannot consume eval data without ad-hoc adapters. The single-baseline / aggregate-cost commitment forecloses the P11 design space less than per-trace or per-criterion cost would, which is appropriate at P5 close (the substrate commits to less ambition than its eventual consumers will need; the consumers extend the substrate when they arrive).

- **D59: Langfuse trace-ingestion asynchrony — polling-with-timeout at the cost-query path** (Package P5, Session S18, 2026-05-06)
  - Choice: Cost queries that may target recently-emitted traces wait for trace availability via polling. ``LangfuseHTTPTraceQueryAdapter.wait_for_trace_availability(trace_id, tenant_context, *, timeout_seconds=30.0, poll_interval_seconds=1.0)`` polls ``get_trace`` (which applies the tenant.id span-attribute filter per D50) until the trace becomes queryable or the timeout elapses; returns True on availability, False on timeout. The CLI runner at S18 invokes this helper between OTel force-flush and the first cost query, once per unique trace_id produced by the replay flow. The 30-second default is conservative for CLI flows; programmatic consumers can tune via parameters. Polling uses ``get_trace`` (not the lower-level ``_fetch_trace``) so cross-tenant probes time out without leaking existence information — a deliberate trade-off that costs one round-trip more per legitimate poll relative to a bare existence check, but preserves D24's tenant-isolation discipline at the polling surface.
  - Reasoning: Langfuse trace ingestion is asynchronous from OTel emission. The OTLP receiver writes to ClickHouse and a worker pipeline materialises the queryable trace on a separate cadence. Consumers that emit a trace and immediately query for its cost (the CLI runner's replay → cost-aggregate flow) hit a race window between OTel export and Langfuse ingestion. S17b worked around this with an 8s force-flush-and-sleep that is brittle: insufficient under variable load (peak ingestion lag can exceed 8s and produce false-empty cost results that the operator misreads as "qwen at zero rates" rather than "trace not yet ingested"); wasteful when ingestion is fast (the 8s sleep is paid even when the trace is queryable in 1s). Polling-with-timeout is honest: it waits exactly as long as needed for fast ingestion and surfaces a clear False return that the caller can treat as a clear failure. The 30-second default reflects the upper bound of the dev-environment ingestion window observed across the S17b-S18 development arc; production tenants with higher ingestion volume tune up, batch-mode programmatic consumers tune down. The two-phase rendering alternative (immediate report with cost-pending sections, follow-up render when costs land) was rejected for CLI flows because the CLI is a one-shot operation; the operator wants a complete report or a clear error, not a partial result they have to re-query. The two-phase shape makes sense for streaming UI surfaces at P11 but not at S18. The webhook/push alternative (Langfuse pushes "trace ready" events to Padhanam) was rejected because it requires Langfuse-side configuration not in the case study's control, and breaks D27's vendor-portability posture (any backend swap would need to re-implement the push).
  - Alternatives considered: Sync wait at a fixed sleep duration (rejected: brittle under variable Langfuse load; 8s might not be enough at peak ingestion lag and is wasteful at quiet times; this is exactly the S17b shape that surfaced the asynchrony problem and motivated D59). Two-phase rendering with cost-pending sections (rejected: complicates the CLI surface; one-shot CLI operations want complete results; the two-phase shape makes sense for streaming UI surfaces at P11 but not at S18). Webhook/push from Langfuse (rejected: requires Langfuse-side configuration not in the case study's control; breaks D27's vendor-portability posture; would force re-implementation on every backend swap). Polling without tenant filter (rejected: cross-tenant probes would leak existence information through the polling-availability response; the tenant.id filter on ``get_trace`` keeps the polling consistent with the cost-query path's tenant-isolation discipline). Polling at the application-layer use case rather than the adapter (rejected: the polling shape is vendor-quirk-specific to Langfuse's async-ingestion behaviour; a backend that materialises traces synchronously needs no polling. Adapter-level placement keeps the vendor knowledge confined per D27). Adding ``wait_for_trace_availability`` to the ``TraceQueryPort`` Protocol (rejected at S18: only the Langfuse adapter needs it at one consumer; promoting to the port for one consumer is paper architecture, and the recommendation engine at P11 will likely need a different shape — recently-traces-since-checkpoint or watch-for-new-trace — that warrants its own port method when the consumer arrives. If a second polling consumer arrives without a different shape, port promotion lands at that session's framing).
  - Kano: must-have. Without a documented asynchrony handling, every cost-query consumer reinvents the pattern, and the case study posture cannot honestly claim "cost-aware optimization recommendations" at P11 if cost-query results are race-condition-prone. The S17b 8s force-flush-and-sleep already exposed the fragility; D59 commits the architectural shape that consumers from S18 forward inherit. The structural-promotion threshold from the methodology applies asymmetrically here: this is a corrective discipline-addition (S17b's brittle workaround surfaced a real failure mode), so it lands on first observation rather than waiting for recurrence — same shape as the methodology's Failure-modes entries that land on single instance per the discipline-vs-pattern asymmetry.

- **D60: P6 source-ingestion framing — asynchronous pipeline shape, one ingestion bounded context** (Package P6, P6-open strategic block, 2026-05-07)
  - Choice: P6 ships source ingestion as an asynchronous pipeline behind a single bounded context at ``contexts/ingestion/`` covering upload, parsing, pipeline orchestration, embedding, graph extraction, indexing, and retrieval (per D16's bounded-contexts-on-hexagonal shape). Upload requests return immediately with a source identifier and an initial status; background workers run the pipeline stage by stage, and the source row carries a per-stage status field that drives reentrancy. Worker writes are designed to be idempotent: re-running the same stage against the same source produces the same result without duplication. The queue mechanism is Postgres-native via ``SELECT ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`` against pending source rows; no new infrastructure dependency lands at P6 (no Redis Streams, no RabbitMQ). The CLI surface lands at ``apps/cli/`` per the workspace-member precedent set at S18, with subcommands under ``padhanam ingest``. The API endpoint shape settles at the implementing session. Two architectural questions defer to implementation: Neo4j topology (per the corresponding entry in `deferred-decisions.md`) and within-tenant segmentation primitive (kept in framing-conversation history only, no charter commitment). Tenant isolation discipline holds for both pgvector and Neo4j however the topologies land, verified by extension of the existing ``tests/contract/tenant_isolation/`` harness per D24.
  - Reasoning: Bursty concurrency is the load profile for tenant onboarding (50 documents arriving in 10 minutes, then nothing for hours). Synchronous request-response is architecturally wrong against that profile because it holds connections open while the inference pipeline runs and pegs throughput against connection count rather than worker count. Asynchronous shape with a queue absorbs the burst into worker capacity and scales by adding workers, which is the operator-credible scaling story at any deployment context. Per-stage status on the source row plus idempotent worker writes is the lightest mechanically-honest shape for two-track coordination across pgvector and Neo4j: writes are not transactional across stores, saga compensation is heavier machinery than P6 scale warrants, and the outbox pattern's discipline (idempotency at the write level) is the underlying requirement regardless of which superstructure sits above it. Postgres ``SKIP LOCKED`` is mature, well-understood, and avoids new infrastructure cost at Phase 1 scale; it remains a credible production-deployment shape at expected tenant ingestion volumes. One bounded context covers upload, pipeline, and retrieval because the natural splitting seam if it ever needs it later is the worker boundary, which lives inside one context cleanly today; two contexts at P6 scale is paper architecture per the methodology's structural-promotion threshold pattern. Deferring Neo4j topology and within-tenant segmentation matches the methodology's commitment-needs-evidence posture: Phase 1 has two test tenants and toy corpus data; framing-altitude commitment to either choice is reasoning into a position rather than validating one, the same shape of overreach the operator caught in the framing conversation.
  - Alternatives considered: Synchronous request-response pipeline (rejected: bursty concurrency makes the connection-holding shape architecturally wrong; pegs inference throughput; bad UX for documents that take minutes to process; aborts whole pipeline on any single-stage failure). Saga with cross-store compensation (rejected: paper architecture at P6 scale; undo operations are themselves writes that can fail and produce partial-undo states; idempotency at the write level is the underlying discipline regardless of whether a saga sits above it). Outbox table with separate consumer service (rejected: adds infrastructure surface beyond what Phase 1 needs; the source row itself plays the outbox role through per-stage status columns). Redis Streams as queue substrate (rejected at P6: new infrastructure dependency for a workload Postgres ``SKIP LOCKED`` handles cleanly; D18 already defers Redis topology decisions until production posture is known). RabbitMQ or Kafka as queue substrate (rejected at P6: heavier infrastructure than Phase 1 scale warrants; deferred until measured demand justifies the substrate change). Two bounded contexts splitting source storage from pipeline (rejected: paper architecture at P6 scale; the natural splitting seam is inside one context at the worker boundary). Three bounded contexts splitting upload, pipeline, and retrieval (rejected: same reasoning, worse). Committing to Neo4j topology at framing (rejected: no evidence pressure-tests either choice; framing-prompt-as-recommendation pattern lands the choice at the implementing session). Pre-committing schema room for within-tenant segmentation tags (rejected: no real consumer at Phase 1; commits the architecture to a model that has not been tested with real corpus or real cross-functional retrieval patterns; retrofittable when a real consumer arrives at P8 or later).
  - Kano: must-have. The pipeline shape is the architectural foundation P6's later sessions inherit; without a documented commitment, every later session reinvents the coordination model. The bounded-context shape gates the directory layout and import-linter contract surface for P6. Both commitments are foundational rather than performance or delighter.

- **D61: Parsing scope at P6 close — markdown and plain text at S19; PDF, DOCX, HTML defer** (Package P6, Session S19, 2026-05-07)
  - Choice: A ``Parser`` port at ``contexts/ingestion/ports/parser_port.py`` defines a single ``parse(source: Source) -> ParsedContent`` contract returning a tuple of chunk strings plus structural metadata. Two adapters land at S19: ``MarkdownParser`` using ``markdown-it-py>=3.0`` (token-based AST that exposes heading hierarchy as a stream of tokens, cleanest for heading-boundary splitting) producing chunks split on heading boundaries with hierarchy preserved as metadata (heading text, heading level); ``PlainTextParser`` producing chunks split on paragraph boundaries (double-newline separators, with whitespace-only paragraphs filtered out). Parser dispatch is by file extension at the ``parser_dispatch`` use case in the application layer. Unsupported formats produce a clear validation error (``UnsupportedFileTypeError``) at the upload-side use case (``register_source``) before the worker pulls the source. PDF, DOCX, and HTML defer to sessions with real consumers — the choice is recorded as forward-affordance in the file_type CHECK constraint, which lists only ``markdown`` and ``text`` at S19.
  - Reasoning: Markdown plus plain text is the smallest scope that exercises the structure-aware-vs-flat chunking distinction and the extension-dispatch seam meaningfully without committing to PDF parsing complexity at S19. PDF and DOCX add real dependency surface (``pypdf``, ``pdfplumber``, ``python-docx``) and structure extraction is non-trivial against either format; landing them at S19 buys complexity ahead of consumer evidence. The parser-as-port shape commits the structural seam at S19; format coverage extends incrementally as consumers arrive (a P7 or P8 tenant onboarding session that ships PDF-as-corpus is the natural trigger). ``markdown-it-py`` over ``markdown`` (Python-Markdown) and ``mistune``: ``markdown-it-py`` is CommonMark-compliant, exposes a token-based AST that walks cleanly for heading-boundary splitting (the ``open``/``close`` heading tokens carry the level so a single pass over the token stream produces ordered chunks with heading hierarchy as metadata), and is the canonical CommonMark parser in the Python ecosystem (myst-parser, sphinx-myst, JupyterBook all build on it). Python-Markdown converts to HTML which is one transformation removed from what ingestion needs; mistune is fast but has a less-standard token API. The dependency-cost difference between the three is modest at the ingestion adapter; the API ergonomics for heading-boundary chunking justify markdown-it-py.
  - Alternatives considered: Markdown only at S19 (rejected: trivial without plain text; doesn't exercise extension dispatch; the PlainTextParser surfaces the chunking-shape distinction the parser-as-port abstracts over, making the seam meaningful at S19). PDF at S19 (rejected: heavy dependency surface; structure extraction is non-trivial against PDF's variable document shape; defer until consumer evidence justifies). HTML at S19 (rejected: same reasoning as PDF; HTML ingestion overlaps with web-scraping concerns out of P6 scope). DOCX at S19 (rejected: same reasoning as PDF; python-docx parses cleanly but the structure-extraction story is identical work to do twice for two consumers that don't exist yet at S19). Chunking by token count rather than structural boundaries (rejected: structure-aware chunking preserves semantic units that retrieval quality depends on; token-count chunking is a downstream refinement option for S20+ when the embedding adapter's token-budget pressure surfaces; the current commitment is structural-first, with token-budget overflow as a future S20 refinement). Python-Markdown over markdown-it-py (rejected: HTML conversion is one transformation removed from what ingestion needs; AST extraction from HTML adds parser indirection where markdown-it-py's tokens are direct). mistune over markdown-it-py (rejected: smaller dependency but less-standard token API; the ecosystem alignment with markdown-it-py — myst-parser, sphinx-myst — pays back when documentation-corpus ingestion arrives at later packages).
  - Kano: must-have on the parser-as-port shape (gates every later parsing extension; without the port the next format addition is a refactor of the application layer rather than an adapter addition). Performance on specific format coverage (markdown plus text is the floor; richer corpus support is performance against a richer retrieval surface).

- **D62: Embedding model default and chunk-embedding column shape — nomic-embed-text via Ollama, single column on chunks, HNSW cosine** (Package P6, Session S20, 2026-05-07)
  - Choice: The default embedding model is ``nomic-embed-text:v1.5`` served via Ollama, accessed through LiteLLM's OpenAI embeddings API path (``/v1/embeddings``) so the gateway routing reuses the inference adapter's HTTP infrastructure. The chunk's embedding lives as a single ``embedding vector(768)`` column on the existing ``chunks`` table per per-tenant Alembic revision ``0006_add_chunk_embedding`` (nullable so a chunk row can exist before embedding lands and so the embedded vs non-embedded shape is observable on the row); an HNSW index ``chunks_embedding_hnsw_idx`` using ``vector_cosine_ops`` with pgvector defaults (``m=16, ef_construction=64``) lands in the same migration. ``default_embedding_model`` joins ``InferenceSettings`` in ``padhanam/config/inference.py`` with default ``nomic-embed-text:v1.5``, parallel to the existing ``default_model`` for generative inference. The pricing table at ``padhanam/config/inference.py`` extends with the embedding model entry (zero rates for the Ollama-hosted dev model, honest per the D49 precedent for ``qwen2.5:7b``); embedding cost flows through the existing trace-attribution path per D41 with no new architectural surface. The ``SourceState`` enum extends with ``embedding``, ``embedded``, ``embedding_failed``; the worker polls for ``parsed`` state via a new ``claim_pending_for_embed`` repository method, claims the source under SKIP LOCKED, embeds chunks via UPSERT on ``chunks.id`` for idempotent re-embed, and transitions source state through ``embedding`` to ``embedded``. The LiteLLM model_list extends with a ``nomic-embed-text:v1.5`` entry (``model: ollama/nomic-embed-text:v1.5``, ``model_info.mode: embedding``) on the gateway. Build-time refinement on the model card requirement: ``nomic-embed-text:v1.5`` requires a task-prefix token on the input (``search_document:`` for corpus-side embeddings, ``search_query:`` for query-side); the LiteLLM adapter prepends ``search_document:`` to chunk content at embed time, recorded as a build-time refinement of the brief's framing.
  - Reasoning: ``nomic-embed-text`` has the longest track record of credible Ollama-served embedding models, 768-dim is a reasonable storage cost in pgvector at Phase 1 corpus sizes (an HNSW index over 768-dim float vectors is operationally cheap at <100k rows), the OpenAI-API shape means LiteLLM routing reuses the inference path the chat adapter already uses (D27 portability holds; no new vendor surface enters the codebase), and the smaller parameter count keeps inference cost tractable on the constrained dev hardware (S6 reflection: ~19 tok/s on Qwen 2.5 7B as the local-laptop ceiling). Single-column shape on chunks keeps worker logic and the pgvector index simple; flexibility for multi-model A/B testing has no consumer at Phase 1 and the migration to a separate ``chunk_embeddings`` table is cheap if a real consumer arrives. HNSW plus cosine matches pgvector's recommended default for higher-dimensional retrieval workloads, matches nomic's recommended distance metric for the v1.5 model, and pgvector's documented ``m=16, ef_construction=64`` defaults are appropriate at Phase 1 corpus sizes (the README explicitly recommends defaults unless recall is low). Building the index in the schema migration rather than after bulk-load is honest at Phase 1 scale (the chunk corpus is empty when the migration runs); the production-bulk-load posture defers to production deployment context. The ``embedding`` column lives on ``chunks`` rather than a parallel table because the embedding is per-chunk by definition and the FK relationship is 1:1; flexibility for "this chunk has multiple embeddings under different models" has no Phase 1 consumer and would force every retrieval query through a join even though no query needs the multi-model branch. SourceState extension on the ``sources`` table (not on ``chunks``) keeps the source-row-as-pipeline-state-of-record discipline D60 commits to: chunks are derivative artefacts, sources are the lifecycle entity. Worker polling adds a second-stage claim method (``claim_pending_for_embed``) rather than generalising to ``claim_pending_in_state(state)`` because the claim semantics differ per stage (the embed claim transitions PARSED→EMBEDDING; the parse claim transitions RECEIVED→PARSING) and per-stage methods read more clearly than a parameterised one — the S19 forward-affordance reflection named both options, build chose the per-stage method.
  - Alternatives considered: ``mxbai-embed-large`` (rejected: marginally better benchmarks at meaningfully higher inference cost on constrained dev hardware; revisit if eval harness surfaces retrieval-quality problems specific to nomic). ``bge-m3`` (rejected: stronger multilingual coverage than nomic but heavier inference cost; Phase 1 corpus is English-only and the multilingual lift is unrealised optionality). Separate ``chunk_embeddings`` table with FK to chunks (rejected: paper architecture at Phase 1, multi-model A/B testing has no consumer, separate-table migration is cheap if needed later, every retrieval query would pay an extra join even when no query needs the multi-model branch). IVFFlat index instead of HNSW (rejected: HNSW is pgvector's recommended default for higher-dim retrieval with better query performance at higher accuracy; IVFFlat requires periodic re-clustering as the index ages and the per-tenant tuning surface is a worse cost than HNSW's per-query ``ef_search`` tuning; HNSW survives more gracefully under heterogeneous corpus growth). L2 or inner-product distance (rejected: nomic-embed-text v1.5 recommends cosine; mismatching the model's recommended metric trades retrieval quality for no architectural gain). Embedding via direct Ollama call bypassing LiteLLM (rejected: breaks D27's portability boundary; LiteLLM is the gateway and embedding traffic should flow through it for the same trace-attribution reasons chat traffic does). Generalised ``claim_pending_in_state(state)`` method on the repository (rejected at build: the per-stage claim methods read more clearly and the differential claim semantics — different source-state-transition per claim — are honest about the per-stage shape rather than hiding it behind a parameter). Bulk-loading chunks then building the HNSW index after-load (rejected at Phase 1: chunk table is empty when the migration runs; the after-load build pattern is the production posture and lands when production deployment context arrives). UPSERT on ``(source_id, chunk_index)`` rather than ``id`` for the embedding write (rejected: ``id`` is the primary key, the UPSERT semantics are cleaner against PK; ``(source_id, chunk_index)`` is the UNIQUE that prevents duplicate row creation but the embedding update is per-chunk-row, ``chunks.id`` is the right key for the UPSERT). Token-count attribution bridge in the embedder adapter via a local tokenizer (rejected at S20: documented as forward-affordance because the cost-attribution gap surfaces in build but the bridging logic adds vendor surface — local tokenizer dep — that has no other consumer; the ``gen_ai.cost.pricing_status`` attribute extends with an ``embedding_no_token_count`` value to surface the gap honestly without breaking the cost path).
  - Kano: must-have on both halves. Embedding model default gates every later retrieval evaluation (P6 retrieval session; P11 retrieval-quality recommendation surface); chunk-embedding column shape gates the schema and pgvector index strategy that S22 retrieval queries against and that the production-deployment-context bulk-load posture inherits.

- **D63: Neo4j topology — shared instance with tenant-property scoping enforced through a tenant-scoped session wrapper plus contract tests** (Package P6, Session S21, 2026-05-07)
  - Choice: P6 ships a single Neo4j 5 Community instance shared across all tenants. The Compose stack adds one ``padhanam-neo4j`` service (Neo4j 5 Community pinned per D10) with bolt and HTTP endpoints used by all tenants. Tenant isolation is enforced at three layers: (1) **Property-based scoping at the data layer** — every ``:Entity`` node and every relationship carries a ``tenant_id`` property that matches both endpoint nodes for relationships; uniqueness constraints include ``tenant_id`` so the same name/entity_type combination across tenants produces independent nodes. (2) **Tenant-scoped session wrapper at the routing layer** — a single ``TenantScopedNeo4jSession`` wrapper at ``contexts/ingestion/adapters/outbound/neo4j/`` owns the bolt driver session and is the *only* surface that exposes Cypher execution to the rest of the codebase. The wrapper is constructed with a ``TenantContext`` and exposes domain-shaped methods (``merge_entities``, ``merge_relationships``, ``get_entities_for_source``, ``get_relationships_for_source``); each method's Cypher template is parameterised on ``$tenant_id`` from the bound context and the wrapper auto-binds the predicate. Raw ``neo4j.AsyncDriver.session()`` and ``tx.run()`` calls live only inside this wrapper module. An import-linter contract ``neo4j-confined`` (and an AST enforcement test at ``tests/_enforcement/test_no_raw_neo4j_session.py``) forbids ``import neo4j`` outside ``contexts/ingestion/adapters/outbound/neo4j/`` and forbids any direct ``driver.session()`` or ``tx.run()`` call outside the wrapper module, so missing-predicate Cypher cannot exist in callable code. (3) **Tenant-isolation contract tests** in ``tests/contract/tenant_isolation/`` extending the harness from D24: red-team-shaped tests attempt cross-tenant graph access through the GraphRepository adapter and assert the access fails on both reads (tenant A's session cannot read tenant B's entities) and writes (a write under tenant A's context that names a tenant-B-scoped entity by uniqueness key produces a new tenant-A node, not a cross-write). This is an explicit deviation from D32's per-instance Postgres posture, recorded with reasoning here.
  - Reasoning: Neo4j Community Edition does not support multiple databases the way Postgres instances do; the equivalent of D32's per-instance posture would require one Neo4j container per tenant, costing roughly 1GB RAM per tenant in local dev and scaling operational complexity linearly with tenant count. At Phase 1 corpus sizes the isolation discipline is what matters and contract tests verify it; per-instance posture is procurement-credible but pays meaningful local-dev cost without yet earning back proportional security or operational benefit. The tenant-scoped session wrapper is what makes property-based scoping mechanically defensible rather than discipline-dependent: the property predicate is not "every developer remembers to add ``WHERE n.tenant_id = $tenant_id`` to every Cypher query" (which decays under maintenance pressure exactly the way ``import-linter``-less hexagonal contexts decay) but "every code path that reaches the bolt driver goes through a wrapper that constructs the Cypher string with the predicate already bound, and a CI-enforced structural test fails the build if any code path bypasses the wrapper." Reversible if production deployment context surfaces a need to upgrade to per-tenant containers; the deferred-decisions entry "Per-tenant topology for Neo4j" stays in the file as the activation marker for that revisit, with framing language updated to reflect that S21 settled the Phase 1 choice.
  - Alternatives considered: Per-tenant Neo4j containers (rejected at Phase 1 scope: ~1GB RAM per tenant in local dev, linear operational complexity, no Phase 1 evidence that property-based isolation is insufficient; revisit at production-deployment context). Defer-again to production (rejected: pushes a decision that has to land somewhere onto a future session that may lack full context too; lands the call where the implementation forces it, consistent with the framing-prompt-as-recommendation pattern). Neo4j 5 Enterprise multi-database (rejected: licensing cost, contradicts local-first principle, premature commitment to enterprise tier without evidence that property-based scoping fails). Per-tenant labels (e.g. ``:Entity_tenant_a``) instead of properties (rejected: label proliferation at high tenant counts; schema-explosion is a real operational concern at hundred-tenant deployments; property-based scoping is the more flexible posture and cleanly supports tenant-id-as-runtime-parameter rather than tenant-id-as-schema-element). String-search test on Cypher literals (rejected as the enforcement mechanism: fragile against string-construction patterns, multi-line strings, helper-built Cypher; the structural test on the wrapper boundary catches the same failure mode mechanically rather than textually). Per-query operator discipline ("every query includes the predicate, reviewed at PR time") without the wrapper (rejected: operator-discipline-as-architecture is exactly the failure mode D24's red-team contract tests, D27's adapter-confinement contracts, and the AST enforcement tests across P3 were promoted to mechanically prevent; the tenant predicate has the same load-bearing position and warrants the same mechanical enforcement).
  - Kano: must-have. The topology gates the Compose service shape, the routing layer's query construction, the migration approach for graph schema, and the tenant-isolation contract tests. The wrapper-plus-import-linter shape gates the structural enforcement that makes property-based scoping defensible at audit.

- **D64: Graph extraction shape — Qwen 2.5 7B via LiteLLM structured output, single ``:Entity`` node, free-form ``entity_type``, no taxonomy commitment** (Package P6, Session S21, 2026-05-07)
  - Choice: Graph extraction over parsed chunks reuses the inference default model (``qwen2.5:7b`` per D15) via LiteLLM's function-calling / structured-output mode (the build session settles whether tool-calling against Qwen 2.5 7B via Ollama is reliable; if not, the deviation lands in this D-entry's Choice text per the framing-prompt-as-recommendation pattern). A new ``EntityExtractor`` port at ``contexts/ingestion/ports/entity_extractor.py`` defines the contract ``extract(chunks: Sequence[Chunk]) -> ExtractionResult``; the LiteLLM adapter at ``contexts/ingestion/adapters/outbound/extraction/litellm_extractor.py`` calls the same gateway path the embedding and inference adapters use, with a prompt that yields ``{entities: [...], relationships: [...]}`` as JSON. The graph schema at S21 commits to a single ``:Entity`` node label with properties ``tenant_id``, ``jurisdiction``, ``name``, ``entity_type`` (free-form string), ``source_chunk_ids`` (List<String>), ``created_at``. Relationships are typed Neo4j edges (the relationship-type comes from the extraction prompt; no taxonomy commitment) with properties ``tenant_id``, ``jurisdiction``, ``source_chunk_id``, ``created_at``. Entity uniqueness is keyed on the composite ``(tenant_id, name, entity_type)`` for idempotent re-extraction via Cypher ``MERGE``. Relationship uniqueness is keyed on ``(tenant_id, source_id, target_id, relationship_type, source_chunk_id)``. No commitment to a taxonomy of entity types or relationship types; the extraction prompt produces whatever set fits the corpus, the build session settles the small initial set the prompt produces, taxonomy refinement happens when a real consumer demands it. Additional node labels (e.g. ``:Regulation``, ``:System``, ``:Verdict``, ``:Obligation``) for future use cases explicitly do not land at S21; the architecture does not foreclose them, and the data-retrieval design session inherits the question of richer graph shapes and projection patterns. Extraction cost flows through the existing trace-attribution path per D41 with no new architectural surface; the LiteLLM adapter emits the GenAI semantic-convention attributes plus the four ``gen_ai.cost.*`` attributes plus the three ``tenant.*`` attributes the inference and embedding adapters established at S15 and S20.
  - Reasoning: Reusing ``qwen2.5:7b`` matches D15's single-default-model posture for inference, avoids new model dependencies on constrained dev hardware, and keeps the LiteLLM gateway as the single inference surface (D27 portability holds). Single ``:Entity`` node label with free-form ``entity_type`` is the conservative shape: it ships extraction without committing to a taxonomy that might be wrong for the use cases the platform encounters; multi-node-label architectures are additive at the schema layer (adding a new label is a Cypher operation, not a migration) and not foreclosed. The composite-key uniqueness ``(tenant_id, name, entity_type)`` is human-readable for debugging and audit and supports Cypher MERGE directly; opaque ``entity_id`` uniqueness keys would force a separate identity-resolution mechanism without measurable benefit at Phase 1. ``MERGE`` semantics give idempotent re-extraction: re-running the extractor over the same chunks produces no duplicate nodes (tenant_id+name+entity_type matches the prior MERGE) and no duplicate relationships (the five-component relationship key prevents duplicate edges between the same nodes from the same chunk).
