Rejected because the bet's success criteria 2 and 4 reach completeness with P9-P12 backend in Phase 1; the substrate-and-methodology reading of the bet that motivated the Phase-1-closes-at-P8 framing was a defensible audit-posture interpretation but not a structural necessity. With Phase 2 direction committed to UX-only per D93, Phase 1 substrate completion is the cleaner shape.

(b) Keep P9-P12 as originally scoped with UI inclusive. Rejected because Phase 2's UX-purity commitment under D93 forecloses; UI elements naturally belong in the Phase 2 consumer-grade surface, where the audience and design constraints are coherent.

(c) Defer some of P9-P12 to Phase 2 (e.g., P10 alone, or P12 alone). Rejected on dependency-chain grounds for P11 and P12; rejected on audit-evidence grounds for P10.

(d) Split the audit into mid-phase work (now) plus end-of-phase work (at P12 close). Rejected because the audit's value compounds against complete delivery; metrics review against incomplete data, role-function audit against incomplete role-function exercise, and bet load-bearing claims review against incomplete delivery all weaken when split. Mid-phase artefact refresh (methodology v2, PRFAQ v3, deck v2) defers to the P12 close audit alongside the rest.

**Kano.** Must-have. The Phase 1 scope confirmation is foundational; without it, the audit at P12 close has no clear measure of what Phase 1 was supposed to be, and the P9-P12 framing conversations cannot inherit a clear UI-versus-backend boundary.

## D93: Phase 2 direction is methodology-as-product positioning with focus purely on UX/UI (Phase 1 / Phase 2 boundary strategic block, 2026-05-13)

**Choice.** Phase 2 direction is methodology-as-product positioning per the PRFAQ candidate framing, with Phase 2 work focusing purely on UX/UI on top of Phase 1's complete substrate. Methodology-as-product means the discipline pattern productised: the gallery surface (browsable roles and methodologies), methodology adoption flows, agent-authoring UX for consumers, run-history-as-learning surface, methodology library UI, audit-and-trust surface, optimisation-recommendation UX, active-testing reports UX. The bet's `bet.md` line 67 anticipated commercialisation path ("L&D, advisory, content, and senior-role positioning rather than a product company") is honoured. The intelligence-layer positioning per D82 lands at UX altitude; the architecture's anticipation of consumer-grade UX (role-first model per D86, methodology overlay with binding modes per D81 and D87, coach consistency, safety surface per D82, light UX as authoring discipline) becomes the substrate UX is built on.

Phase 2 sequencing within methodology-as-product follows three waves: (a) consumer-facing UX first — gallery, methodology adoption flow, agent-authoring surface, run-history-as-learning surface, methodology library UI; (b) trust-and-evidence layer second — audit-and-trust surface for tenant operators, active-testing reports; (c) optimisation UX third — the procurement-grade recommendation surface that closes bet success criterion 4. The optimisation UX has a different audience inside Phase 2 (senior product leaders, tenant operators, procurement readers) than the consumer-facing first wave (methodology adopters); both audiences sit inside methodology-as-product at different roles. Specific Phase 2 package shape lands at the Phase 1 close audit's Phase 2 framing conversation; this entry commits the direction only.

**Reasoning.** Three candidate Phase 2 framings surfaced in `charter/prfaq.md`: platform-as-product positioning with first commercial tenants; methodology-as-product positioning with the discipline pattern productised; deepening the optimisation layer for an internal-only research vehicle. The bet's load-bearing differentiator is consumer-expectance-with-enterprise-guardrails (the NotebookLM-plus-Gems framing in the operator's standing instructions, the "methodology runs in the background and surfaces at decision points, not as workflow gates the user is forced to traverse" commitment at `bet.md` line 34, the intelligence-layer positioning at D82). Only methodology-as-product satisfies both dimensions simultaneously. Platform-as-product satisfies enterprise-guardrails strongly but trades against consumer-expectance (the audience is procurement, security, finance, platform engineering) and requires a bet revision per `bet.md` line 69's "Phase 2 may revisit this; Phase 1 does not." Optimisation-layer deepening forecloses the consumer dimension entirely; keeps Padhanam in research-vehicle territory and forecloses the L&D market the bet's audience section commits to.

The decision also clarifies the bet's commercialisation path. `bet.md` line 67 names the anticipated path explicitly: "Selling the methodology is anticipated to take the form of L&D, advisory, content, and senior-role positioning rather than a product company." D93 commits to that path. The Apache 2.0 licensing per D77 holds; the methodology is the proprietary insight; the platform is the public demonstration; commercialisation of the methodology rather than the platform is the path.

Phase 1's substrate-and-architecture commitments either anticipate this Phase 2 framing or were neutral on direction. Role-first identity per D86 anticipates the gallery's role-browsing surface; methodology aggregate v2 per D81 anticipates the methodology adoption flow; intelligence-layer positioning per D82 anticipates the consent-granularity UX surfaces; coach consistency principle anticipates the methodology-as-playbook-the-coach-applies UX. The architecture's anticipation of UX shape per the deferred-decisions cluster (HTTP API for ingestion at UI consumer arrival, HTTP API for evaluation at UI consumer arrival, tenant-facing UI for agent invocation at Phase 2 gallery surfaces) all activate at Phase 2 opening.

**Alternatives considered.**

(a) Platform-as-product positioning with first commercial tenants. Rejected on two grounds. First, consumer-expectance is weak-to-medium under enterprise-admin UX shape; the bet's load-bearing differentiator is not exercised. Second, the framing requires revisit of the no-product commitment per `bet.md` line 69; the bet's structure allows the revisit but does not anticipate it, and the methodology-as-proprietary-insight framing per D77 is structurally inconsistent with platform-as-product.

(b) Optimisation-layer deepening for internal-only research vehicle. Rejected because it forecloses the consumer-expectance dimension entirely. The audience under this framing is engineering teams and procurement, not the senior product leaders the bet's audience section names. Forecloses the L&D market the bet anticipates as the commercialisation path's primary domain.

(c) Defer Phase 2 direction to the Phase 1 close audit at P12 close. Rejected because P9-P12 framing needs Phase 2 direction as input. Framing P9-P12 as backend-only depends on Phase 2 being UX-shaped; deferring Phase 2 direction would mean framing P9-P12 against an unknown audience and use pattern, which forces either defensive paper-architecture in the backend that may not match the eventual UX needs, or under-committed backend scope that gets re-opened during Phase 2 to add missing pieces.

(d) Defer optimisation UX to Phase 3 or later, focusing Phase 2 on consumer-facing UX only. Rejected because optimisation UX is load-bearing for the bet's success criterion 4 ("the trace capture layer surfaces optimization recommendations, not just data"). Without UX, the recommendations exist in the backend at Phase 1 end and never reach a user. The bet does not close.

(e) Combine methodology-as-product with platform-as-product (Phase 2 sells both the methodology and the platform). Rejected because it dilutes both. The methodology-as-product path commercialises L&D, advisory, content, and senior-role positioning; platform-as-product commercialises hosted SaaS for enterprise customers. Both at once requires the bet revision platform-as-product alone would, plus the consumer-grade UX investment methodology-as-product needs; capacity question aside, the two audiences require different product surfaces and different go-to-market motions.

**Kano.** Must-have. The Phase 2 direction commitment foundationally shapes the remaining P9-P12 framing conversations (backend-only with consumer-UX consumers in mind), the Phase 1 close artefact refreshes (PRFAQ v3 voice, deck v2 audience), and the Phase 2 opening at audit close.

## D94: P9 run-history substrate framing; per-tenant Postgres canonical with snapshot citations, single-transaction completion seam, new `contexts/run_history/` bounded context, consumer-defined UX-shaped query port (Package P9, P9-open strategic block, 2026-05-13)

**Choice.** P9 ships the run-history substrate as four committed shapes. First, the substrate is per-tenant Postgres tables: a `runs` table holding the structured run record, a `run_chunk_citations` table linking runs to source chunks via real foreign key plus rendering-grade snapshot columns (chunk excerpt, source citation), a `run_entity_citations` table linking runs to Neo4j entities via the `(entity_tenant_id, entity_name, entity_type)` composite per D64 plus rendering-grade snapshot columns (entity display label). All three tables sit on the tenant's data plane per D32. The trace store (Langfuse via OTel) stays as operational observability; the run record carries `trace_id` as the join key for deep operational drill-down per D27. Second, the event-stream-to-record assembly seam is at invocation completion: the executor derives the run record plus citation rows from the in-flight event stream from S29b and writes them in a single transaction against the per-tenant database. Third, the bounded context for run-history work is new at `contexts/run_history/`, separate from `contexts/agent/`. The agent context calls into run-history at run completion via the consumer-port-plus-wiring-adapter pattern reinforced across S26a through S29b. Fourth, the read-side surface at P9 is a single consumer-defined query port shaped to Phase 2 UX consumption (individual run rendering, run listing with filters, citation surface fetch). P11's aggregation-shaped read port lands at P11 framing per the consumer-defined-ports precedent from D5, D56, and D65. Specific column lists, indexes, constraints, and foreign-key deletion behaviors settle at the implementing sessions per the established schema-discipline norm.

**Reasoning.** D92 committed P9 as backend-only substrate that Phase 2 UX consumes directly per D93's methodology-as-product positioning. The substrate must serve three downstream consumers cleanly: Phase 2 UX for run rendering and citation display, P11 for cross-run aggregation and recommendation generation, ops engineers for operational deep-dives. These three reads have legitimately different shapes; pushing them through one surface compresses unrelated concerns. Splitting the read paths along consumer lines is the established pattern: ports defined by the consumer that uses them, not speculatively by the producer (D5, D56, D65 precedent across three reinforcements).

The canonical-Postgres-plus-derived-trace-store split absorbs three commitments the charter already made. D92 requires citation linking at the data layer, which forecloses citation-as-OTel-span-attribute approaches and requires database-enforced foreign keys where the destination is Postgres-resident. D27 commits the trace store as a swappable boundary; routing Phase 2 UX queries directly through the trace-store API inverts that boundary. The database-per-tenant principle (D32, standing rule) wants product-grade run records living per-tenant alongside the audit chain they cite into; the trace store is shared infrastructure by nature and serves operational concerns at a different retention cadence.

The snapshot pattern on citation rows commits to audit-evidence-grade historical fidelity. A citation row that says "the agent cited 'Article 9(2)(a)' on 13 May 2026" should hold its meaning even if the underlying chunk is later re-ingested, the source removed, or the entity merged. Denormalising rendering-relevant fields onto the citation row at write time gives Phase 2 UX immediate rendering without back-joining and gives the audit posture a snapshot semantics that the bet's audit-evidence claim earns.

The single-transaction completion seam matches the established discipline. Partial-write run records visible to readers mid-run create consistency problems; out-of-band background derivation creates eventual-consistency problems that Phase 2 UX would feel as "the run finished but does not appear yet." Writing the verdict-grade derivation at invocation completion in one transaction is the simplest correct shape and matches how S29b's audit chain already writes its end-row at invocation exit.

The new bounded context at `contexts/run_history/` reflects different audiences for the agent runtime versus the run-history surface. The agent context's audience is the agent author and the executing agent; the run-history context's audience is anyone wanting to look at what happened (Phase 2 UX, P11, audit reviewers). Different audiences justify different bounded contexts under D16's hexagonal-on-bounded-contexts shape. The cross-context call from agent to run-history at completion is one-time wiring cost paid once at the substrate session.

**Alternatives considered.**

(a) Projection over the trace store with no new per-tenant tables. Rejected because D92's data-layer citation linking requirement forecloses citation-as-span-attribute approaches, D27's trace-store-as-swappable-boundary inverts if Phase 2 UX queries the trace store directly, and the database-per-tenant principle wants product-grade data per tenant rather than in shared trace infrastructure.

(b) Full primary tables in the tenant's database carrying per-iteration and per-LLM-call detail. Rejected because the nested OTel span hierarchy from S29b already captures iteration and LLM-call timing at the granularity the trace store was designed for; duplicating in Postgres writes the same data twice on every run and creates a second query path consumers must maintain for cross-cutting views. The hybrid lean-record-plus-trace-id-pointer shape is the cleaner split.

(c) Single polymorphic citations table with two nullable foreign-key columns and a CHECK constraint enforcing exactly one is set. Rejected because the schema looks unified but the integrity guarantees are heterogeneous: the chunk side gets real foreign-key enforcement, the entity side cannot have a Postgres foreign key to Neo4j. We carry the cost of a polymorphic schema (CHECK constraints, nullable FK pairs, conditional joins) without the unification benefit.

(d) Resolve entity citations to chunk citations at write time via the entity's `source_chunk_ids` property in Neo4j, eliminating the entity citation table entirely. Rejected because D92 explicitly names source chunks and graph entities as separate citation primitives; collapsing entity citations into chunk citations loses the structural evidence that the agent used graph retrieval. The methodology-as-product audience reading the citation surface has a legitimate reason to see graph-traversal citations as graph-traversal citations.

(e) Single unified read API serving both Phase 2 UX and P11. Rejected because UX and aggregation read shapes are structurally different (UX wants get-this-run and list-runs-with-filters; P11 wants distributions and trend lines); a single surface compresses unrelated concerns. The consumer-defined-ports precedent across D5, D56, and D65 lands the same shape for the same reason.

(f) Extending `contexts/agent/` to include run-history concerns instead of creating a new bounded context. Rejected because the run-history audience is structurally different from the agent runtime's audience, and Phase 2 UX importing from `contexts/agent/` to render a runs list would leak agent-runtime vocabulary into UX code without semantic justification.

(g) Building P11's aggregation-shaped read port speculatively at P9. Rejected because P11 has not framed and its read shape is not yet concrete; speculative interface design produces commitments P11 then renegotiates. The consumer-defined-ports precedent says read interfaces land when the consumer that uses them is concrete enough to define them.

(h) Eager projection of run records and citations into Neo4j as a derived view for graph-shape traversals. Held as Phase 2 forward-affordance, not Phase 1 commitment, because Padhanam's Neo4j is primarily a per-tenant retrieval substrate rather than a cross-cutting reasoning graph; projecting run history into it requires also projecting chunks and adds substantive substrate. Phase 2 UX may or may not need graph-shape traversal queries; the commitment lands when a real consumer requires them.

**Kano.** Must-have on the four committed shapes (Shape C tables on the tenant data plane with snapshot citations, single-transaction completion seam, new bounded context at `contexts/run_history/`, UX-shaped consumer-defined read port). These together close P9's contribution to the Phase 1 substrate that D92 commits Phase 2 UX consumes directly. Performance on the rendering-grade snapshot fields specifically (the audit-fidelity benefit and the no-back-join rendering benefit both compound, but the substrate would function without them at higher Phase 2 join cost). Delighter on the trace_id join key (cheap to carry on the run row; gives operational deep-dives a clean entry point without product surfaces needing trace-store knowledge).

## D95: P9 run-history concrete schema for `runs`, `run_chunk_citations`, `run_entity_citations`; FK-deletion-behaviour commitments preserve snapshot fidelity; write-timing commitment yields terminal event before projection write; audit-chain partial-state shape preserves projection for recorded-activity-but-chain-incomplete invocations (Package P9, Session S31, 2026-05-13)

**Choice.** Three per-tenant tables land at S31 via Alembic revision `0011_create_run_history` on the `alembic/tenant/` track, applied to each registered tenant by `make migrate`'s per-tenant phase per D36.

`runs` carries fifteen columns. `id uuid` (PK, default `gen_random_uuid()`). `tenant_id text` (NOT NULL, CHECK `<> ''`, denormalised per D22). `jurisdiction text` (NOT NULL). `agent_template_id uuid` (NOT NULL). `agent_template_version integer` (NOT NULL). `input_message text` (NOT NULL, the user-supplied input echoed for audit and rendering). `output_content text` (NOT NULL, the agent's final content; empty string for `InvocationFailed` and `InvariantBlocked` terminations). `started_at timestamptz` (NOT NULL). `completed_at timestamptz` (NOT NULL). `termination_reason text` (NOT NULL, CHECK in {`content`, `max_iterations`, `tool_not_registered`, `error`, `invariant_blocked`, `failed`}, the five values from the `TerminationReason` enum at `contexts/agent/domain/termination.py` plus a synthesised `failed` value for `InvocationFailed` terminal events whose event class has no `termination_reason` field). `iteration_count integer` (NOT NULL, CHECK `>= 0`). `total_cost_usd numeric` (NOT NULL, CHECK `>= 0`). `trace_id text` (nullable, the OTel trace identifier from the active span at invocation completion, the join key to the trace store per D27; nullable because trace emission failure in dev is non-fatal). `audit_start_hash text` (NOT NULL, 64 hex chars, CHECK length 64, the `this_event_hash` from the audit row at `InvocationStarted` per S29b). `audit_end_hash text` (nullable, 64 hex chars when present, CHECK `audit_end_hash IS NULL OR length(audit_end_hash) = 64`; additional CHECK `(termination_reason = 'failed') OR (audit_end_hash IS NOT NULL)` — only `failed` terminations may carry NULL end-hash, signalling the 1-hash `partial_audit_chain_state` shape from `contexts/agent/adapters/outbound/agent_loop_executor.py` where the start audit row landed but the end audit did not). `created_at timestamptz` (NOT NULL, default `now()`).

Indices on `runs`: `ix_runs_agent_template_id` on `agent_template_id`; `ix_runs_started_at` on `started_at` (Phase 2 UX filter); `ix_runs_trace_id` on `trace_id` WHERE `trace_id IS NOT NULL` (partial index for ops-deep-dive join).

`run_chunk_citations` carries eight columns. `id uuid` (PK, default `gen_random_uuid()`). `run_id uuid` (NOT NULL, FK to `runs.id` ON DELETE CASCADE). `chunk_id uuid` (nullable, FK to `chunks.id` ON DELETE SET NULL so the snapshot survives source removal per D94's audit-evidence claim). `tenant_id text` (NOT NULL, CHECK `<> ''`). `jurisdiction text` (NOT NULL). `chunk_excerpt text` (NOT NULL, the snapshot of chunk content for rendering; specific population semantics deferred to S32 per p9-epic open question). `source_citation text` (NOT NULL, the snapshot of source identification, filename and chunk index, for rendering). `created_at timestamptz` (NOT NULL, default `now()`).

Indices on `run_chunk_citations`: `ix_run_chunk_citations_run_id` on `run_id`.

`run_entity_citations` carries eight columns. `id uuid` (PK, default `gen_random_uuid()`). `run_id uuid` (NOT NULL, FK to `runs.id` ON DELETE CASCADE). `entity_tenant_id text` (NOT NULL, CHECK `<> ''`, matches the Neo4j entity's `tenant_id` property per D63). `entity_name text` (NOT NULL, matches Neo4j entity's `name` property). `entity_type text` (NOT NULL, matches Neo4j entity's `entity_type` property, free-form per D64). `tenant_id text` (NOT NULL, CHECK `<> ''`, denormalised on the row per D22; identical to `entity_tenant_id` but the routing key for the per-tenant database). `entity_display_label text` (NOT NULL, the snapshot of entity rendering label; population semantics deferred to S32). `created_at timestamptz` (NOT NULL, default `now()`).

Indices on `run_entity_citations`: `ix_run_entity_citations_run_id` on `run_id`.

The `(entity_tenant_id, entity_name, entity_type)` composite is the documented join key back to the Neo4j entity per D64's uniqueness commitment. No Postgres foreign key is possible because the destination is Neo4j-resident; the snapshot columns carry the rendering payload that survives entity merge or removal per D94's audit-evidence claim.

Write-timing within `invoke_agent`. The use case's async generator yields the terminal event from the executor's stream before calling `writer.record_run(record)`. The writer call follows the yield; the generator returns after the writer call completes. Shape: update accumulators per event, yield event, then if the event is a terminal type, assemble the record and write. Writer failure raises post-yield, after the SSE client has received the runtime's terminal event.

Audit-chain partial-state mapping at write time. The executor at `contexts/agent/adapters/outbound/agent_loop_executor.py` produces `InvocationFailed.partial_audit_chain_state` with three shapes depending on where the failure landed: empty tuple (pre-start-audit failure), single-element tuple (post-start-audit-but-pre-end-audit failure, the loop-body-exception and end-audit-emission-failure sites), two-element tuple (post-end-audit forward-affordance variant, currently not fired by the executor). `invoke_agent`'s assembly logic maps these to the runs row as: empty → skip the writer call entirely (no runs row, consistent with the projection-over-recorded-activity framing for invocations with no audit evidence); single-element → write a runs row with `audit_start_hash=partial[0]`, `audit_end_hash=NULL`, `termination_reason='failed'`; two-element → write a runs row with `audit_start_hash=partial[0]`, `audit_end_hash=partial[1]`, `termination_reason='failed'`. The `InvocationCompleted` event class supplies `audit_chain_hashes: tuple[str, str]` populated with both hashes; the `InvariantBlocked` event class supplies the same pair shape; both produce runs rows with both hashes NOT NULL.

**Reasoning.** The fifteen-column `runs` shape captures the rendering-relevant state Phase 2 UX consumes (lineage via `agent_template_id` plus version, the user input and agent output for the run-detail surface, time bounds, termination outcome, cost, iteration count) plus the join keys for ops drill-down (`trace_id` to the trace store, audit hashes to the audit chain). Snapshotting `input_message` and `output_content` on the runs row rather than referencing through to a separate messages table earns the audit-fidelity-by-default property D94 commits: a runs row read at retrieval time renders identically to the run as executed, regardless of how the rest of the substrate evolves. The lineage fields (`agent_template_id`, `agent_template_version`) reference the live `agent_templates` table without snapshotting the agent's content because `agent_templates` is append-only per D75 (archived rows stay; templates do not get deleted), so the FK-equivalent relationship survives any operational state the template enters.

The FK-deletion-behaviour commitments are the load-bearing schema choice. ON DELETE CASCADE on `run_id` for both citation tables is correct because a citation has no meaning without its parent run; if a run is deleted the citations cascade. Runs are append-only audit evidence at Phase 1; deletion is operationally rare, governed by retention policy when production lands. ON DELETE SET NULL on `run_chunk_citations.chunk_id` is the snapshot-fidelity commitment in action: when a source is removed and its chunks deleted, the citation row stays with its `chunk_excerpt` and `source_citation` snapshot columns intact, only the FK reference becoming NULL. Phase 2 UX can render the citation faithfully from snapshot columns; the broken FK is structural evidence to the audit reviewer that the underlying source no longer exists, which is itself useful audit signal.

The denormalised `tenant_id` on every table per D22 makes each row self-describing for cross-database aggregation queries (when those land) and provides defence-in-depth alongside D32's per-tenant database routing. The CHECK `tenant_id <> ''` constraint mirrors the per-tenant `tenant_audit` precedent from S12.

The write-timing commitment (yield terminal event before writing the projection) flows from D94's framing of the runs row as the rendering projection over the canonical audit chain. Three reasons converge. First, D94 explicitly frames the audit chain as the canonical record and the runs row as the projection; collapsing the two by making the terminal event semantically equivalent to "row exists" forces the runtime's terminal-event contract to absorb a persistence-success guarantee the executor never had. Second, the executor's terminal-event contract from D90 (one of `InvocationCompleted`, `InvocationFailed`, `InvariantBlocked`) is authoritative about what happened in the runtime; raising a persistence-layer exception into the runtime's event-stream surface would force the SSE client to receive errors that are not about agent execution, muddying the event vocabulary D90 committed at maximum granularity. Third, the asymmetry between fine-grained traces and simple audit that D90 commits explicitly (different resolution needs for operational versus compliance questions) generalises here: audit is the compliance record; the runs row is the consumer projection; failure modes for the two surfaces are not the same. Phase 2 UX must handle the missing-row case at consumption time by reconciling against the audit chain via `audit_end_hash`; the reconciliation cost is bounded because the audit row carries the same `agent_template_id` and `tenant_id`, so the missing-projection case is "audit row exists without matching runs row → surface as 'run executed but projection missing'" rather than silent drift.

The audit-chain partial-state mapping at write time preserves the projection for recorded-activity-but-chain-incomplete invocations while honoring the projection-over-recorded-activity framing. The S31 reconciliation finding at session-open against `agent_loop_executor.py` surfaced three concrete shapes the executor produces for `InvocationFailed.partial_audit_chain_state` (empty, single-element, two-element); the schema and write-time logic mirror the structural distinction: empty → no runs row (no audit evidence, no projection target); single-element → runs row with NULL end-hash and `termination_reason='failed'` (audit evidence exists, chain incomplete, projection rendered with chain-incomplete signal); two-element → runs row with both hashes and `termination_reason='failed'` (forward-affordance for the documented but currently-unfired post-end-audit-exception variant). The nullable `audit_end_hash` is scoped tightly via the CHECK `(termination_reason = 'failed') OR (audit_end_hash IS NOT NULL)` so non-failed terminations cannot accidentally carry NULL; the relaxation surface is bounded to the failure-mode case the executor's taxonomy actually produces.

**Alternatives considered.**

(a) ON DELETE CASCADE on `chunk_id`. Rejected because cascade deletion of citation rows on source removal loses the snapshot evidence D94 commits to preserve. The audit-evidence-fidelity claim that earned D94's Shape C requires the snapshot survives the deletion.

(b) ON DELETE RESTRICT on `chunk_id`. Rejected because blocking source removal when citations exist couples retention policy to citation history; tenant operators legitimately need to remove sources and Phase 2 UX should not require pre-citation cleanup as a prerequisite for source management.

(c) No FK on `chunk_id`, store as opaque uuid only. Rejected because the FK preserves referential clarity while the row is live (which is most of the time at Phase 1 scale) and the SET NULL semantics gives the same long-term snapshot survival as no-FK without the up-front loss of referential integrity.

(d) Single `citations` table polymorphic on a `citation_type` discriminator covering both chunks and entities. Rejected per D94's alternative (c) reasoning: the integrity guarantees are heterogeneous (FK on chunks, no FK on entities), polymorphism carries the cost of CHECK constraints and conditional joins without the unification benefit.

(e) Storing `input_message` and `output_content` as compressed BYTEA. Rejected because Phase 1 corpus sizes do not justify compression overhead and TEXT columns are readable through `psql` for operational deep-dives.

(f) `total_cost_usd` as integer cents rather than NUMERIC. Rejected because LiteLLM's cost surface returns Decimal per D49 and the precision lossless-round-trip from Decimal to NUMERIC matches the D49 precedent.

(g) Carrying a CHECK that exactly mirrors the `TerminationReason` enum (five values: `content`, `max_iterations`, `tool_not_registered`, `error`, `invariant_blocked`) without the synthesised `failed` value. Rejected because the `InvocationFailed` terminal event class has no `termination_reason` field; collapsing it onto `error` (the closest enum value) loses the handled-versus-unhandled distinction (`TerminationReason.ERROR` is set when the loop completes with an error result; `InvocationFailed` is emitted when an unhandled exception aborts the loop). The six-value CHECK preserves the distinction at the projection surface; the synthesis is the runs-row-only mapping per S31 reconciliation.

(h) `audit_start_hash` and `audit_end_hash` as BYTEA. Rejected because the audit-chain hex-string convention from D22 / D37 mirrors as TEXT-of-64-hex-chars across all chain-touching code paths; introducing a different shape at the run-history table creates a translation point with no benefit.

(i) Write the runs row before yielding the terminal event (shape A: writer-success implies row-exists at terminal-event-receipt). Rejected because the shape collapses the asymmetry D94 commits between canonical audit chain and rendering projection, forces the runtime's terminal-event contract to absorb a persistence guarantee the executor never had, and surfaces persistence-layer exceptions into the runtime's event-stream surface where they muddy the D90 event vocabulary. The Phase 2 UX reconciliation cost of shape B (missing rows reconcilable from audit) is bounded and aligned with D94's projection framing; the runtime-contract cost of shape A would compound across future consumers.

(j) Schema-strict `audit_end_hash NOT NULL` with skip-writer-call for any `InvocationFailed` with `len(partial_audit_chain_state) < 2`. Rejected because it silently drops all post-start-audit failures (the 1-hash case the executor currently produces at the loop-body-exception and end-audit-failure sites in `agent_loop_executor.py`) from the runs surface at S31; Phase 2 UX would not see failed runs that produced audit evidence, undermining the projection-over-recorded-activity framing. The nullable shape with CHECK pairing preserves the projection for invocations that DID produce activity while honestly signalling the chain-incomplete state.

(k) Treat `InvocationFailed` events as the existing `TerminationReason.ERROR` value on the runs row and lose the handled-versus-unhandled distinction. Rejected because the executor structurally distinguishes the two cases at emission time; Phase 2 UX needs the distinction at the projection surface, and collapsing them at the runs row would force a chain-walk against the audit table to recover the distinction Phase 2 UX needs.

**Kano.** Must-have on the column lists, FK-deletion behaviours, the write-timing commitment, and the audit-chain partial-state shape. The runs schema is the minimum surface Phase 2 UX consumes; the FK behaviours are the load-bearing audit-fidelity commitment D94 names; the write-timing commitment preserves the executor's terminal-event contract from D90 against persistence-layer impedance; the partial-state shape preserves the projection for recorded-but-incomplete invocations without forcing schema-strict semantics that drop them. Performance on the three indexes (Phase 2 UX queries benefit from the `started_at` filter at higher run counts; the partial `trace_id` index serves ops without imposing cost on the dominant non-trace-bearing rows). Delighter on the `trace_id` partial index (cheap to carry; gives ops deep-dive a clean entry point without product surfaces needing trace-store knowledge per D94's existing delighter framing on the `trace_id` field itself).

## D96: Citation surface — agent-context-owned candidate types, ToolCallCompleted event extension, single-transaction multi-table write, source_snapshot JSONB schema revision, within-run deduplication (Package P9, S32, 2026-05-13)

**Choice.** Citation linking from agent runtime to the per-tenant run-history substrate lands at four committed shapes that together turn S31's citation-tables-exist-but-unpopulated state into an end-to-end citation surface from retrieval through to per-tenant Postgres rows.

First, the agent context defines two citation candidate value objects at `contexts/agent/domain/citation_candidates.py`. `ChunkCitationCandidate` carries `chunk_id: UUID`, `source_id: UUID`, `chunk_index: int`, `content_snapshot: str`, `source_snapshot: Mapping[str, Any]`, `tenant_id: str`, `jurisdiction: str`; invariants reject empty `tenant_id`, empty `jurisdiction`, negative `chunk_index`, and empty `content_snapshot`. `EntityCitationCandidate` carries `entity_tenant_id: str`, `entity_name: str`, `entity_type: str`, `source_chunk_ids: tuple[UUID, ...]`, `tenant_id: str`, `jurisdiction: str`; invariants reject empty `entity_tenant_id`, empty `entity_name`, empty `entity_type`, empty `tenant_id`, empty `jurisdiction`, and `entity_tenant_id != tenant_id`. A discriminated union `CitationCandidate = ChunkCitationCandidate | EntityCitationCandidate` is the field type the event carries.

Second, `ToolCallCompleted` at `contexts/agent/domain/events.py` gains `citation_candidates: tuple[CitationCandidate, ...] = ()`. Default empty preserves backwards compatibility; tools producing attributable output fill the field. The retrieval tool is the only Phase 1 producer; future tools (web fetch, document parse, structured extraction) extend the same field. `ToolInvocationResult` at `contexts/agent/application/ports/tool_invoker.py` gains the same-shaped field so the `ToolInvoker` port surface carries citation candidates from the wiring adapter to the executor; the executor reads the field at the tool-call yield site and copies it onto `ToolCallCompleted`.

Third, the schema at `run_chunk_citations` revises from `source_citation text NOT NULL` to `source_snapshot jsonb NOT NULL DEFAULT '{}'::jsonb` to keep render shape (Harvard, footnote, hover card, et al.) as a Phase 2 read-time concern over a structured input snapshot. At Phase 1 the snapshot carries `{"file_name": <str>, "file_type": <str>}`; richer ingestion enrichment fills more fields without schema change. The schema at `run_entity_citations` drops `entity_display_label text` (synthesised at render time from name + type) and adds `source_chunk_ids text[] NOT NULL DEFAULT '{}'::text[]` to snapshot the provenance trail from the Neo4j entity back to per-tenant Postgres chunks; the audit-evidence-fidelity argument from D94 holds for entity provenance the same way it holds for chunk content. Alembic revision `0012_revise_citation_snapshots` on the `alembic/tenant/` track lands the column changes; the citation tables are empty at S32 session-open across both tenants (verified) so the revision is structural-only.

Fourth, `PostgresRunHistoryAdapter.persist` grows its `async with session.begin()` block to write runs + chunk citations + entity citations within a single transaction. Partial failure rolls all three back; the runs row only persists if both citation inserts complete (or are no-op for empty tuples). The accumulator at `invoke_agent` deduplicates citation candidates within the run by `(chunk_id, run_id)` for chunks and `(entity_tenant_id, entity_name, entity_type, run_id)` for entities; first-seen snapshot wins. The deduplication is at accumulation time inside `invoke_agent`, not at write time inside the adapter, because the structural invariant (no duplicate citation rows per run) lives at the projection-assembly layer where the run identity is composed; the adapter's role is to write the assembled projection.

The `AgentRetrievalClient` consumer port at the agent context extends its return shape from `tuple[RetrievedChunk, ...]` to a `RetrievalResult` envelope carrying `chunks: tuple[RetrievedChunk, ...]` (the LLM-facing projection) and `citation_candidates: tuple[CitationCandidate, ...]` (the citation surface). The `AgentRetrievalClientAdapter` at `apps/cli/_cross_context.py` produces both projections from the same ingestion-side `ChunkResult` and `EntityResult` so the translation is single-pass. The ingestion-side `ChunkResult` extends to surface `chunk_index: int` and `source_snapshot: Mapping[str, Any]` (populated from the `sources` join with `file_name` and `file_type` at Phase 1); the pgvector SQL grows two columns in the `SELECT` to project them. The `RunRecord` domain object at the run-history context extends with `chunk_citations: tuple[ChunkCitationRecord, ...]` and `entity_citations: tuple[EntityCitationRecord, ...]`; the run-history-side record types mirror the agent-side candidate types one-for-one; the wiring adapter does the DTO-to-domain translation per the D54 precedent.

**Reasoning.** The four shapes hang together on three load-bearing arguments. First, storage-versus-render separation: D94's audit-evidence-fidelity claim holds at storage time independent of any render commitment Phase 2 UX makes. `source_snapshot jsonb` over the prior `source_citation text` keeps render shape as a Phase 2 concern over a structured input snapshot; `source_chunk_ids text[]` over the prior `entity_display_label text` makes the entity provenance trail load-bearing audit evidence rather than a pre-rendered display field. The asymmetry surfaces in maintenance terms: a Phase 2 render shape change (Harvard to footnote) under the prior schema would require backfilling the `source_citation` column across the historical citation set; under the JSONB schema the change is read-time only. Second, audit-evidence-fidelity over render-commitment-at-storage: every chunk the retrieval tool surfaces enters the citation accumulator regardless of whether the LLM cites it in the final answer. The argument from the strategic conversation: there is no honest signal at Phase 1 distinguishing "retrieved-and-used" from "retrieved-and-discarded" — the LLM does not emit a structured discard signal; trying to derive one from the response heuristically would itself be unreliable. Audit-floor commitment lands the row for every retrieved chunk; consumer evidence at Phase 2 refines if discard-explanation tooling becomes necessary. Third, chunk-level granularity sufficient at Phase 1: extraction-style tools (the case where sub-chunk precision would matter) are not in the Phase 1 tool catalogue; deferring sub-chunk precision until the forcing function arrives keeps the substrate's invariant surface small. The within-run deduplication policy is `(chunk_id, run_id)` and `(entity_tenant_id, entity_name, entity_type, run_id)`; the within-run scope reflects that a chunk retrieved twice within the same run is still one piece of evidence for that run, while the same chunk retrieved across separate runs is independent evidence for each run.

The single-transaction multi-table write is the natural extension of D95's shape-B write-timing commitment at S31. The seam landed cleanly at S31's runs-only path; growing it to three tables preserves the same property (terminal event yields first, then atomic write commits) while adding the multi-table atomicity guarantee Phase 2 UX needs for consistent citation rendering. Partial failure rolls all three tables back so the substrate never carries half-written projections; either the run + all its citations land together, or none of them do. The audit chain remains the canonical record per D94 in both cases.

The candidate types live at the agent context per D17's consumer-defined-ports precedent: the agent context defines what it needs from the citation surface (a structured candidate type the tool invocation result can carry); the run-history context defines what it persists (the record types mirroring the candidate types); the wiring adapter at `apps/cli/_cross_context.py` does the translation. The structural duplication (candidate types at agent context, record types at run-history context) is the intentional cost of the D17 boundary; the alternative (single shared type at `shared_kernel/`) was rejected per the D54 precedent that mirror types at context boundaries earn the boundary's independence. The discriminated union over chunk and entity candidates surfaces the two structurally distinct citation kinds without polymorphism on a `citation_kind` discriminator; per-type invariants stay tight and per-type rendering at Phase 2 UX is straightforward to dispatch.

**Alternatives considered.**

(a) Keep `source_citation text` and commit a render format now (e.g., "{file_name}:{chunk_index}"). Rejected per the storage-versus-render argument: a Phase 2 render shape change would require a backfill across the historical citation set. The structured JSONB shape accommodates any future render decision without schema work.

(b) Leave `entity_display_label text` and synthesise the label at write rather than read. Rejected per the same argument: render-shape changes (case-folding, type-prefix-versus-suffix, Harvard-style entity formatting) would force a backfill. The `(entity_name, entity_type)` join key plus the `source_chunk_ids` provenance array gives Phase 2 UX everything needed to render any shape.

(c) Discriminated single candidate type with a `citation_kind` field over a polymorphic dataclass. Rejected because per-type invariants (chunk requires `chunk_index >= 0`; entity requires `entity_tenant_id == tenant_id`) become conditional validations on a single type; the union of two frozen dataclasses keeps the invariant surface per-type-tight and per-type-rendering straightforward.

(d) Record only what the LLM cites in its final answer rather than everything retrieved. Rejected per the LLM-does-not-emit-discard-signal honesty argument and the audit-floor commitment. The retrieved set is the honest evidence the platform stands behind; trying to filter heuristically would introduce unreliability the audit surface cannot tolerate.

(e) Discard-explanation layer with a second model pass (LLM analyses its own answer and explains which retrieved chunks it used versus discarded). Rejected per research-territory and Phase 1 scope arguments: the discard-signal-from-LLM problem is not solved at the platform-substrate level; building a second-pass layer at Phase 1 commits to a research outcome the platform does not need.

(f) Sub-chunk precision (carry character offsets or sub-paragraph spans on the citation row) now rather than waiting for extraction tools to enter the picture. Rejected per no-Phase-1-tool-produces-this-case argument: retrieval is the only Phase 1 producer; chunk-level granularity is what retrieval surfaces; sub-chunk precision lands when extraction-style tools enter and a real consumer demands the granularity.

(g) Write the runs row first and the citation rows in a second transaction. Rejected because partial failure between the two transactions would leave runs without citations or citations referencing a missing run; the single-transaction commitment preserves the projection's structural integrity.

(h) Deduplicate at the adapter (write-time) rather than at the accumulator (assembly-time). Rejected because deduplication is a property of the projection-assembly layer where run identity is composed; the adapter's role is to write the assembled projection, and pushing deduplication into the adapter would couple persistence to projection logic in a way that complicates the read-side path at S33.

(i) Carry citation candidates on every tool-related event (`ToolCallProposed`, `ToolCallExecuting`, `ToolCallCompleted`) rather than only `ToolCallCompleted`. Rejected because the candidates are computed at tool-invocation-completion; carrying empty tuples on the proposed/executing events would muddy the event vocabulary without value.

(j) `source_snapshot` as a flat `jsonb` map versus a typed Pydantic shape. Chose `jsonb` map for Phase 1 because the field set evolves as ingestion enrichment lands (NotebookLM-style metadata, OCR confidence, parse provenance, et al.) and a typed shape would couple the citation table to ingestion's evolving metadata catalogue. Phase 2 UX reads the JSONB shape via documented keys; a typed shape can land at Phase 3 when the metadata catalogue stabilises.

**Kano.** Must-have on the candidate types and the event extension and the schema revisions; these are the substrate Phase 2 UX reads and P11 optimisation joins to. Must-have on the single-transaction multi-table write; partial failure tolerance is the structural-integrity floor Phase 2 UX needs for consistent rendering. Performance on the within-run deduplication policy (the differentiator for citation surface usability; consumer evidence at Phase 2 refines the policy if necessary). Delighter on the structured JSONB `source_snapshot`'s anticipation of NotebookLM-style ingestion enrichment without schema change; the `source_chunk_ids` array's preservation of entity provenance for cross-store audit chains.

## D97: P9 run-history read surface — RunHistoryReader port, RunRecord-as-aggregate, four-filter list vocabulary, cursor pagination, storage-versus-render discipline at read surface (Package P9, S33, 2026-05-13)

**Choice.** P9 ships the run-history read surface at four committed shapes that together turn S32's write-side citation surface into a queryable read surface for the eventual Phase 2 UX consumer.

First, the query port `RunHistoryReader` lives at the run-history context's `contexts/run_history/ports/reader.py` with two methods. `get_run(tenant_context, run_id) -> RunRecord | None` returns the run plus its chunk citations plus its entity citations as one aggregate, or None when the run-id is not present on the routed tenant. `list_runs_with_filters(tenant_context, filters, cursor) -> RunListPage` returns a page of runs (no citations attached at list-view altitude) plus an optional next-page cursor. The port lives at the producer context per the S22 `RetrievalClient` precedent because the consumer (an eventual HTTP API at S34 or S35) is a composition surface rather than a bounded context; the port shape is informed by Phase 2 UX consumption per D93/D94 in the consumer-defined-shape sense.

Second, the read DTO is `RunRecord` itself — the same aggregate type the writer adapter persists. `RunRecord` already carries `chunk_citations: tuple[ChunkCitationRecord, ...]` and `entity_citations: tuple[EntityCitationRecord, ...]` per D96; the read adapter populates the tuples from the joined query at `get_run` time and leaves them empty at `list_runs_with_filters` time. The `source_snapshot` JSONB stays as `Mapping[str, Any]` at the DTO boundary; the consumer projects what it needs at render time. No rendered fields, no projected fields, no computed presentation hints at the port surface. The read DTO is symmetric with the write side: the same record type travels both directions; no read-side wrapper sits between `RunRecord` and the consumer. The S33 reconciliation finding that surfaced this shape (the brief's drafted `RunWithCitations` wrapper would have duplicated fields already present on `RunRecord`) reinforces the storage-versus-render discipline by eliminating a structurally redundant indirection rather than introducing one.

Third, the filter vocabulary on `list_runs_with_filters` commits to four dimensions via a `RunListFilters` value object at `contexts/run_history/domain/query_filters.py`: `agent_template_ids` (multi-value UUID match), `agent_template_versions` (multi-value integer match, applied alongside `agent_template_ids`), `started_at_range` (inclusive lower, exclusive upper datetime tuple), `termination_reasons` (multi-value text match against the D95 six-value CHECK set). Empty tuples are treated as no-filter rather than match-nothing. Sort order is fixed at `started_at DESC, id DESC` (the `id` tiebreaker stabilises pagination at equal-timestamp rows). No other sort orders at S33. Citation tuples at read-time `get_run` are sorted by `id ASC` for stable rendering — the domain records carry `id` but not `created_at`, and `id` is deterministic per row even though it is not chronological at Phase 1's UUID-v4 generation.

Fourth, pagination commits to cursor-based on `(started_at, id)` tuple comparison rather than offset. The cursor is opaque to consumers; the adapter serialises as base64-encoded JSON carrying `started_at`, `id`, and `page_size` via the encode/decode helpers at `contexts/run_history/application/cursor.py`. The query uses `WHERE (started_at, id) < (cursor.started_at, cursor.id)` for subsequent pages and `LIMIT page_size + 1` so the adapter detects whether a next page exists. The page-size ceiling settles at 50 at S33; raise if Phase 2 UX surfaces a credible case for more. A `MalformedCursorError` domain exception at `contexts/run_history/domain/query_filters.py` raises on base64 / JSON / schema / type errors so the HTTP layer at S34/S35 can translate to 400 cleanly.

**Reasoning.** The four shapes hang together on four load-bearing arguments. First, storage-versus-render discipline at the read surface: D96 committed the discipline at storage layer (`source_snapshot` JSONB, `source_chunk_ids` array); the read surface honours the same discipline by returning domain types and refusing to project to render shape at the port boundary. The Phase 2 UX consumer is the right altitude for render decisions; the read port preserves render optionality the same way the storage layer preserves it. Second, bounded citation cardinality justifies the aggregate-as-`RunRecord` shape over separate methods: citations are single-digit per run at Phase 1 retrieval; the FK with cascade delete makes the single-query join cheap; the consumer's run-detail use case wants both together; the separate `get_citations_for_run` method adds round-trips without value at Phase 1 cardinality. Third, cursor over offset for stable scroll-back: list view is a time-ordered stream that gets new rows from concurrent invocations; offset pagination drifts under inserts while cursor on `(started_at, id)` stays stable; the cursor cost is slight extra wiring on the adapter side, the benefit is the structural correctness Phase 2 UX needs for any time-ordered list. Fourth, four-filter set as the minimum credible Phase 2 UX surface: `agent_template_id` (filter to the agent), `agent_template_version` (filter to the version family), `started_at` range (filter to a time window), `termination_reasons` (filter to outcomes including failures). Cost, iteration_count, trace_id are P11 aggregation territory or operational rather than product; jurisdiction is deferred until multi-jurisdiction tenants surface; multiple sort orders compound port-surface area without commensurate value at Phase 1.

Read DTO symmetry with the write side eliminates a wrapper. The brief's drafted `RunWithCitations` carrying `run: RunRecord, chunk_citations, entity_citations` would have introduced a wrapper duplicating fields already on `RunRecord` (from S32's D96 extension). The reconciliation finding surfaced the redundancy at session-open before any code landed; the structurally honest answer reuses `RunRecord` directly. The `RunRecord` shape is the storage record from D95 and the aggregate carrier from D96 and the read aggregate from D97 — a single type at all three altitudes. Future read consumers that need a non-aggregate projection (e.g., a list-view detail flag that signals "this run has citations" without loading them) settle the per-call shape via filters or a separate port method at the session where the consumer use case lands.

Port location at the producer context. The producer-side-port-with-consumer-shape pattern from S22 (ingestion's `RetrievalClient`) applies rather than the consumer-side-port-with-cross-context-wiring pattern from S31 (agent's `RunHistoryWriter`). The distinguishing factor is whether the consumer is a bounded context (S31's case: agent context consumes run-history; port at agent) or a composition surface (S33's case: HTTP API at apps/api consumes run-history; port at run-history). The HTTP API at S34/S35 will dependency-inject the reader from the composition root; no cross-context wiring adapter is needed because the consumer is not a context.

Missing-run None return rather than raise. The HTTP API at S34/S35 translates None to 404; raising at the port surface forces the HTTP layer to catch domain exceptions for what is structurally an expected-empty case. The same shape generalises to other read-port methods where empty-result is a normal outcome rather than an error.

Wiring at composition roots symmetric with the writer. The reader instantiates at `apps/cli/_cross_context.py` (the `RunHistoryReaderAdapter` factory class) and at `apps/api/_agent_runtime_wiring.py` (the `build_run_history_reader` factory function alongside `build_agent_runtime_composition`) even though no consumer calls it at S33; the wiring is the substrate the HTTP layer at S34/S35 dependency-injects against. The symmetry with the writer wiring makes the future HTTP layer's reading and writing patterns visually parallel.

**Alternatives considered.**

(a) Separate `get_citations_for_run(run_id) -> CitationsBundle` method alongside a citation-less `get_run(run_id) -> RunRecord` (with `chunk_citations=(), entity_citations=()`). Rejected because citation cardinality is bounded at Phase 1 (single-digit per run for retrieval) so the round-trip cost of a separate method is the dominant consideration; the consumer's run-detail use case wants both together; adding a method speculatively against a use case that does not yet exist commits surface area future consumers must maintain. Add the separate method when a real consumer use case demands citations without the run.

(b) Returning rendered or projected DTOs at the port surface (e.g., `chunk_excerpt_html`, `entity_display_label_synthesised`, `source_snapshot.file_name` projected onto a top-level field). Rejected because storage-versus-render discipline at S32 committed render shape as a Phase 2 read-time concern; projecting at the port surface re-imports the discipline-violation D96 spent the schema revision to escape. The consumer projects.

(c) Offset pagination rather than cursor. Rejected because the list-view access pattern is "scroll back in time" against a stream that receives concurrent inserts; offset pagination shifts under inserts so a user paging back finds rows they already saw or skips rows they have not seen. Cursor on `(started_at, id)` is stable under inserts because the comparison is against the row's actual sort-key values, not against an offset count.

(d) Combined `RunListQuery` DTO carrying filters, cursor, and sort in one object. Rejected because sort is fixed at S33 (no need to parameterise), filters and cursor have legitimately different lifecycles (filters reset on user-driven filter change; cursor advances on pagination), and the combined object compresses two concepts that would surface as two parameters at the HTTP layer anyway.

(e) Including cost, iteration_count, trace_id, jurisdiction as filters defensively because the indexes exist or are cheap to add. Rejected per consumer-defined-ports precedent: filter surface earns its place when a real consumer use case demands it. Cost and iteration_count are P11 aggregation territory (the "expensive runs" or "runs that looped a lot" patterns are diagnostic, not browsing); trace_id is operational rather than product; jurisdiction earns its place when multi-jurisdiction tenants surface. Adding filters defensively writes tests and indexes for surfaces nobody uses.

(f) Raising on missing run rather than returning None. Rejected because empty-result is structurally an expected case at the read surface (run-id from a stale link, run-id from a different tenant, run-id that was retention-purged); the HTTP layer translates None to 404 cleanly; raising forces every consumer to catch a domain exception for the expected-empty case.

(g) Multiple sort orders (`cost DESC`, `iteration_count DESC`, `completed_at DESC`) at S33. Rejected because Phase 1 has a single credible Phase 2 UX use case for sort (newest first); the additional orders compound port-surface area, query-plan considerations, and pagination correctness (every sort order needs its own cursor tiebreaker) without commensurate value. Add when a real consumer use case demands.

(h) Port location at the consumer side (HTTP API at apps/api) following S31's `RunHistoryWriter` precedent. Rejected because the consumer is not a bounded context: apps/api is a composition surface where multiple bounded contexts' application services compose into HTTP routes. Ports live at bounded contexts; composition surfaces dependency-inject through ports. The S22 `RetrievalClient` precedent is the right match.

(i) Consolidated `PostgresRunHistoryAdapter` implementing both `RunHistoryRepositoryPort` (writer) and `RunHistoryReader` (reader). Defensible if the project's convention were one-adapter-per-technology, but reconciliation at S33 settled on a separate `PostgresRunHistoryReader` at `contexts/run_history/adapters/outbound/postgres/reader.py` for reader/writer separation: read patterns optimise differently (query plans, connection pooling, read-replica routing when production lands); separating now keeps the future optimisation surface uncluttered.

(j) Page-size ceiling at 100 or 200 rather than 50. Rejected at 50 as the Phase 1 default because the Phase 2 UX run-list view is a time-ordered stream the user scrolls; 50 fits a credible scroll-page; larger page sizes compound transfer cost and HTTP-payload size without value at Phase 1 corpus sizes. Raise when a credible case surfaces.

(k) `RunWithCitations` wrapper carrying `run: RunRecord, chunk_citations, entity_citations` as separate fields. Rejected at S33 reconciliation as structurally redundant: `RunRecord` already aggregates the citation tuples from D96, and the symmetry-with-write-side argument (the writer adapter persists `RunRecord` carrying citations; the reader returns `RunRecord` carrying citations) becomes a tautology. The wrapper would create a "thin" indirection that wraps a type that already has the same fields; future maintenance would have to keep the wrapper and the record in sync. Reusing `RunRecord` directly is the structurally honest read-side aggregate shape.

(l) Sort citations at read time by `created_at` rather than `id`. The brief's drafted shape used `created_at`, but `ChunkCitationRecord` and `EntityCitationRecord` do not carry a `created_at` field (the column exists on the table but not on the domain record because S32's record types were write-shaped). Adding `created_at` to the domain records is a one-field extension but introduces a field with no current consumer (the consumer renders citations as a bounded set, not chronologically). `id` is always present, deterministic per row, and stable across reads — the structurally minimum sort key for stability at Phase 1 single-digit citation cardinality. Add `created_at` to the domain records if a Phase 2 UX consumer surfaces a real chronological-rendering use case.

**Kano.** Must-have on the two methods, the `RunRecord`-as-aggregate read DTO shape, the four-filter set, cursor pagination, the missing-run None return, and the `started_at DESC` sort. These together close P9's read-side contribution to the Phase 1 substrate that D92 commits Phase 2 UX consumes directly. Performance on the page-size ceiling at 50 (the default works for the dominant scroll pattern; raising is a tuning question Phase 2 evidence answers). Delighter on the bound-tenant-id defence-in-depth check at both read methods (cheap to add; gives a structurally-honest assertion against cross-tenant data leakage at every read). Indifferent on whether the adapter consolidates reader and writer; the convention call settled at reconciliation in favour of separation per alternative (i).

## D98: P9 run-history HTTP read surface — response DTOs mirror RunRecord 1:1, six-parameter query vocabulary, error response body shape with eleven-path map, principal-derived tenant context per S29b (Package P9, S34, 2026-05-14)

**Context.** S33 landed `RunHistoryReader` plus its Postgres adapter plus composition-root wiring; the reader is inert from a UI consumer perspective until an HTTP transport surfaces it. P9 epic commits Phase 2 UX as the downstream consumer per D93; HTTP is the layer that turns the reader from internal substrate to consumable surface. D98 settles four shapes that together expose the reader as an authenticated, paginatable, filterable web surface.

**Choice.** Four shapes commit at S34.

First, the response DTO at the HTTP boundary mirrors the run-history domain records one-for-one. Pydantic v2 response models at `apps/api/routers/_run_history_dto.py` carry `RunResponse` (every field of `RunRecord`), `ChunkCitationResponse` (every field of `ChunkCitationRecord`), `EntityCitationResponse` (every field of `EntityCitationRecord`), and `RunListResponse` (`runs: list[RunResponse], next_cursor: str | None`). No flattening, no rendering decisions; the storage-versus-render discipline from D96 holds at the HTTP boundary unchanged. Citation tuples on `RunRecord` surface as JSON arrays per Pydantic v2's default tuple-to-list serialisation. `source_snapshot` JSONB surfaces as `dict[str, Any]`. `Decimal` cost serialises as string per the existing inference response convention. `model_validate(run_record)` round-trips cleanly through `ConfigDict(from_attributes=True)`.

Second, the query parameter vocabulary on `GET /runs` is six query params translating to `RunListFilters` + `RunListCursor`. `agent_template_id` (repeated, `Annotated[list[UUID] | None, Query()]`, maps to `RunListFilters.agent_template_ids`); `agent_template_version` (repeated `list[int] | None`, maps to `RunListFilters.agent_template_versions`); `started_at_after` and `started_at_before` (paired `datetime | None` each, both-or-neither, lower-strictly-earlier-than-upper, maps to `RunListFilters.started_at_range`); `termination_reason` (repeated `list[str] | None`, validated against the D95 six-value CHECK set, maps to `RunListFilters.termination_reasons`); `cursor` (opaque base64 string, passed verbatim to the codec); `page_size` (int 1-50 via FastAPI `ge`/`le`). Empty repeated params and `None` both map to `None` (no-filter) per the S33 invariant. A parser dependency at the router module composes the six params into the two domain value objects with all validation upfront so the route handler sees only valid inputs.

Third, the error response body shape carries four fields at `apps/api/_errors.py`: `error_code: str` (machine-readable identifier), `message: str` (human-readable explanation), `correlation_id: str` (per-request UUID from a new `CorrelationIdMiddleware`), `details: dict[str, Any] | None` (field-level validation details for 422s). Eleven verification paths commit: bad UUID path param (422, code `validation_error`); bad query param type (422, `validation_error`); page_size out of bounds (422, `validation_error` via FastAPI ge/le); malformed cursor (400, `malformed_cursor`); started_at_after >= started_at_before or only-one-of-two-dates provided (400, `invalid_filter_range`); missing auth (401, `unauthenticated`, from auth middleware); cross-tenant attempt on `GET /runs/{id}` (404, `run_not_found`, fires `TENANT_SCOPE_VIOLATION` security event because the HTTP layer cannot distinguish cross-tenant from genuine-missing); missing run on `GET /runs/{id}` (404, `run_not_found`, security event fires identically — the indistinguishability is structural); unexpected exception (500, `internal_error`, plus security event if the reader's bound-tenant-id rejection fires — that path should never fire above the data layer); method not allowed (405, FastAPI default body shape); cursor+filters combined verifying the server honours both (200, no error). Cross-tenant `GET /runs` returns empty list with no security event because list-no-results is structurally indistinguishable from no-results-by-filter.

Fourth, principal-derived tenant context follows S29b precedent. The auth middleware sets `request.state.principal`; `get_tenant_context` (reused from `apps/api/routers/inference.py`) resolves the principal's tenant_id against the control-plane registry and constructs `TenantContext`; the route handler depends on the resolved context. Existing dependency behaviour holds: principal whose tenant_id is not UUID-shaped → 400 (`bad_principal`); principal whose tenant is not in the registry → 404 (`tenant_not_found`); both fire from the dependency layer before the route handler runs. This matches every other route's behaviour at the application; introducing a 401 here for the missing-tenant case would diverge from precedent without justification per the brief-versus-built-state reconciliation finding at session-open.

**Reasoning.** The four shapes hang on three load-bearing arguments. First, response-DTO-as-1:1-mirror preserves the storage-versus-render discipline that landed at D96 — the HTTP layer is a transport, not a rendering layer. The Phase 2 UX consumer renders citation excerpts, entity displays, and snapshot fields at its altitude; surfacing those decisions at the API would re-import the discipline-violation D96 spent the schema revision to escape. Second, the eleven-path error map covers every distinguishable failure mode at the route boundary; consolidation (e.g., collapsing the three 400s onto a single `bad_request` code) would lose client diagnosability and force the consumer to parse `message` strings to recover what `error_code` already names. Third, principal-derived tenant context inherits the S29b precedent rather than introducing a new auth pattern at run-history; the cross-tenant 404 firing a security event is the privacy-preserving-plus-auditable shape the principles already commit to, and a consistent shape across the agent and run-history routes reduces the platform's attack surface explanations.

The cursor-and-filter-mismatch policy commits to honouring both independently. Detecting "filter changed since cursor was issued" would require encoding filters into the cursor, which compounds two failure modes: cursor opacity erodes (the cursor would carry filter-shaped information clients could decode and tamper with), and cursor length grows (each filter dimension adds bytes). Client responsibility for consistent filters across paginated calls is the structurally honest contract: the cursor anchors the pagination window; the filter narrows within the window; mismatched filters produce a smaller-than-expected page but never a stale or wrong page. The Phase 2 UX consumer that paginates is the right altitude to enforce consistency at the user-input layer.

Cross-tenant 404 plus security event policy fires on every 404 from `GET /runs/{id}` because the HTTP layer cannot distinguish cross-tenant from genuine-missing without either confirming the existence of a run on a different tenant (which leaks information) or maintaining a tenant-scoped index of run-ids (which adds infrastructure for no product gain). The noise-versus-signal tradeoff: every 404 produces a security log entry, but the entries carry the principal's tenant_id and the requested run_id, so a SIEM downstream can filter by tenant-violation patterns (many 404s from the same principal across distinct run_ids) versus benign-missing patterns (occasional 404s from many principals). The methodology document at Phase 1 close audit may revisit the firing policy if the noise overwhelms the signal in production.

Correlation ID middleware is the operational-forensics seam the brief commits to. The new `CorrelationIdMiddleware` generates `uuid4()` per request, attaches to `request.state.correlation_id`, and returns the value in an `X-Correlation-Id` response header. Exception handlers read from request state to populate the `ErrorResponse.correlation_id` field. Existing routes can pull the value from request state to log it alongside their own structured logs at the next refresh; S34 introduces the middleware and the new error shape simultaneously without retrofitting existing handlers.

**Alternatives considered.**

(a) Reject requests where cursor is present alongside filter params. Rejected because detecting "filter changed since cursor was issued" requires encoding filters into the cursor, which compounds cursor opacity and cursor length costs without correctness gain. Client responsibility for consistent filters is the structurally honest contract.

(b) Flatten citation tuples into a single discriminated citation list at the response shape. Rejected per storage-versus-render symmetry with D96. The domain types carry separate `chunk_citations` and `entity_citations` tuples; the response mirrors that shape; the consumer composes the rendered representation.

(c) Surface JSONB `source_snapshot` as a Pydantic-typed `SourceSnapshot` model with strict field validation. Rejected because the snapshot keys are intentionally schemaless per D96 to accommodate ingestion enrichment evolution (NotebookLM-style extraction may add or remove keys without schema migration); typing the response shape would re-couple the HTTP layer to a key set that should remain open.

(d) Return 403 on cross-tenant `GET /runs/{id}`. Rejected because confirming "this run exists but you don't have access" leaks the existence of the run on another tenant; 404 is the privacy-preserving choice. The principle generalises: any per-tenant resource whose access is gated by tenant identity returns 404 on cross-tenant attempt, not 403.

(e) Fire security events on every list-no-results to maximise audit fidelity. Rejected because list-no-results is structurally indistinguishable from genuine no-results (the user's filter combination genuinely matched no runs on their own tenant). Firing on every no-results would produce noise in the security log that drowns the cross-tenant-attempt signal. The asymmetry between `GET /runs/{id}` (fires on 404 because run-id is specific) and `GET /runs` (does not fire on empty list because filter is broad) is the as-built policy.

(f) Use `tuple[X, ...]` directly in Pydantic response models with custom serialisers preserving the tuple type in JSON. Rejected because Pydantic v2's default tuple-to-list serialisation is the standard JSON shape; consumers expect arrays at the HTTP boundary; preserving tuple-ness at the wire format would require a JSON extension that does not exist.

(g) Inline cursor encoding inside the HTTP layer rather than reusing the codec at `contexts/run_history/application/cursor.py`. Rejected because S33's codec is the established opacity boundary; duplicating logic at the HTTP layer would create drift between the codec's invariants and the route's encoding behaviour. The route accepts the encoded string and decodes via the existing codec; the route returns `next_cursor` as the encoded string via the same codec.

(h) Use FastAPI's `BackgroundTasks` to defer security event firing off the request path. Rejected because tenant-scope violations are operationally urgent — a SIEM downstream needs the events synchronously to fire alerting workflows. The cost of synchronous logging is bounded (a single file append on the local backend; a queued write in production) and well below the route's overall latency budget.

(i) Skip `correlation_id` in the error body if middleware does not already exist. Rejected because forensic correlation across logs, security events, and audit-chain rows requires a per-request identifier; the cost of adding the middleware (one new file at `apps/api/middleware.py`) is bounded; future operations work materially benefits.

(j) Return cursor and page_size in the `RunListResponse` body even when they did not change. Rejected because the cursor is opaque; clients only need `next_cursor` for forward pagination at Phase 1; surfacing the in-flight cursor would risk clients constructing their own cursors rather than treating the field as opaque.

(k) Match the brief's scenario 20 ("principal whose tenant does not exist returns 401") by special-casing the `get_tenant_context` dependency for run-history. Rejected at session-open reconciliation per the brief-versus-built-state finding: the existing `get_tenant_context` at `apps/api/routers/inference.py:113-118` returns 404 from the registry-lookup failure; introducing a per-router special-case for run-history would diverge from the platform's auth-shape consistency. The 404 is the as-built behaviour; the harness scenario 20 asserts 404; the eleven-path error map names the 404 + `tenant_not_found` code.

(l) Mount the run-history router under `/api/v1/runs` rather than `/runs`. Rejected because no other router carries a version prefix (`/agents`, `/inference`, `/tenant`); introducing `/api/v1/` only at run-history would diverge from the platform's URL convention. If the platform commits to versioned URLs at a later phase, the refactor moves all routers together.

**Kano.** Must-have on the response DTO shape (without it the routes are unusable as a Phase 2 UX consumer surface). Must-have on the principal-derived tenant context (without it the routes are insecure or cannot route per-tenant). Performance on the eleven-path error map (a smaller map would technically work but degrades client diagnosability; the cost of the larger map is one exception handler per path, well below the operational benefit). Performance on the six-param query vocabulary (a smaller vocabulary would technically work but degrades filter expressivity Phase 2 UX needs). Delighter on the correlation-id-in-error-body shape (operational forensics improvement beyond functional requirement; the middleware addition is cheap relative to the future operations value).

## D99: Two-tier integration test strategy — default tier excludes real-LLM tests; live-LLM tier runs on opt-in cadence (Package P9 carryover, S35a, 2026-05-14)

**Context.** Top-ten slow integration tests at S35 close run ~695 seconds combined per the durations probe captured at `docs/notes/integration-test-slowness-investigation.md`. The cost is in test bodies executing real LLM inference via LiteLLM against `qwen2.5:7b` plus embedding models against Ollama, not in fixture overhead. Module-scope fixtures are correctly applied; `engine.dispose()` patterns honour module scope. The investigation determined the fix shape is policy, not architecture: separating the tiers preserves test fidelity while restoring fast iteration cycles. The S35 close deferred-decisions entry "Integration-test slowness from real-LLM inference in `tests/integration/`" named the marker-based convention as the recommended shape and deferred the D-entry to the activating session; S35a is that session.

**Decision.** Integration tests exercising real LLM inference carry the `live_llm` pytest marker, applied at file-level via `pytestmark = pytest.mark.live_llm`. The marker is registered in `pyproject.toml` under `[tool.pytest.ini_options].markers`. Default pytest collection excludes the marker via `addopts = "-q -m 'not live_llm'"` in the same section (merging the pre-existing `-q` addopt with the new exclusion). A new Makefile target `make test-live-llm` (and the equivalent `uv run pytest -m live_llm`) runs the live tier explicitly. Operator runs the live tier at per-package close, weekly cadence, or per pre-PR validation; the default tier runs every session-hygiene iteration.

**Reasoning.** Three load-bearing arguments. First, real-LLM tests verify the inference integration end-to-end against the model and embedding behaviour the platform stands behind; mocking them defeats their value, which is precisely the assertion against real model output rather than against a frozen contract. Second, the cost is intentional and load-bearing on what the tests assert; tier separation pays the cost when fidelity is needed and defers it when iteration speed matters. Third, file-level marker placement is structurally robust against new tests added to LLM-exercising files: a future test added to `test_retrieval_e2e.py` inherits the marker without ceremony, while a per-test marker invites omission. The investigation note examined four alternatives and recommended this shape; the additional alternative considered at the D-entry is per-test placement rather than file-level.

**Alternatives considered.** (a) Full mocking of LLM calls; rejected because the tests' value is real-LLM behaviour. (b) pytest-xdist parallelism with per-worker LLM queues; rejected because parallelism does not reduce total cost and adds Ollama-concurrency configuration complexity. (c) Shared-corpus rewrite for retrieval tests; rejected because it requires test-body changes for only partial coverage. (d) Accept the slowness; rejected because session hygiene becomes unworkable at 11-minute test runtimes. (e) Per-test mock-vs-real toggle; rejected as introducing brittle conditional behaviour. (f) Per-test marker placement instead of file-level `pytestmark`; rejected because the per-test approach risks marker omission on new tests added to an LLM-exercising file, while file-level placement is structurally robust.

**Kano.** Performance. The platform works without this but iteration speed materially improves with it.
